By whom are you employed and in what capacity? 
11

12

13

14

15 
3.6 million customers in New York, Massachusetts and Rhode Island.   
16 
 
17

18 
that provide retail gas service to more than 2.4 million customers in three 
19 
service territories: Niagara Mohawk Power Corporation d/b/a National Grid 
20 
(“Niagara Mohawk”) serves areas of eastern and central New York; The 
21

3

4

II. 
Purpose of Testimony 
9 
Q. 
What is the purpose of the Panel’s testimony? 
10 
A. 
The purpose of the Panel’s testimony is to provide the Company’s forecast of 
11 
gas capital investments for the twelve-month period ending June 30, 2022 
12 
(“Rate Year”) and the two subsequent twelve-month periods ending June 30, 
13 
2023 (“Data Year 1”) and June 30, 2024 (“Data Year 2”) (Data Year 1 and Data 
14 
Year 2 are collectively referred to as the “Data Years”).  The Panel discusses 
15 
capital expenditures that will (i) increase the safety and reliability of the 
16 
Company’s gas network, and (ii) modernize the Company’s gas transmission 
17 
and distribution infrastructure to reduce emissions while continuing to meet 
18 
customer needs.  The Panel will also discuss the Company’s practices and 
19 
policies for maximizing the efficiency of its capital delivery program from 
20 
planning and budgeting through the completion of construction. 
21 


--- Page 10 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 8 of 105 
 
 
 
 
1 
 
The Panel’s testimony provides an overview of the significant projects in the 
2 
Company’s capital plan, including retirement of leak prone pipe (“LPP”) and 
3 
new safety programs to identify and address system risks.  The Panel’s 
4 
testimony also presents an overview of the Company’s pipeline integrity and 
5 
reliability programs that will improve the overall safety and reliability of the 
6 
Company’s gas system, and will also address recently enacted, as well as 
7 
pending, pipeline safety regulations administered by the U.S. Department of 
8 
Transportation’s (“DOT”) Pipeline and Hazardous Materials Safety 
9 
Administration (“PHMSA”).  The Panel discusses the Company’s plans and 
10 
infrastructure projects necessary to ensure that the Company can reliably serve 
11 
its existing customers as well as forecast demand for new customer connections 
12 
within the Company’s operating territory. 
13 
 
14 
Q. 
Does the Panel’s testimony also address the Company’s operations and 
15 
maintenance (“O&M”) programs? 
16 
A. 
Yes.  In addition to capital investments in gas infrastructure, the Panel describes 
17 
incremental labor (full time equivalent positions or “FTEs”) and non-labor 
18 
O&M expenses that the Company proposes in the Rate Year and Data Years, 
19 
the costs of which are not fully reflected in the twelve-month period beginning 
20 
January 1, 2019, and ending December 31, 2019 (“Historic Test Year”).  These 
21

Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 9 of 105 
 
 
 
expenses represent known and measurable changes from Historic Test Year 
1 
expenses that are necessary to (i) improve system reliability, (ii) address new 
2 
and emerging safety regulations, (iii) enhance customer service, and (iv) 
3 
support the Company’s capital investments.  The Panel will also discuss the 
4 
Company’s staffing plan for the proposed new FTEs. 
5 
 
6 
Q. 
How has the Company presented its projected capital and O&M 
7 
expenditures in the case? 
8 
A. 
As the Revenue Requirements Panel explains in its direct testimony, the 
9 
Company operates on a fiscal year (“FY”) that runs from April 1 through March 
10 
31, and typically develops its capital and expense budgets on an FY 
11 
basis.  Because of the delay in filing this rate case related to the COVID-19 
12 
pandemic, the proposed Rate Year and Data Years do not directly align with 
13 
the Company’s FY budgeting period. 
14 
 
15 
Unless otherwise specifically indicated, the capital project and program costs 
16 
described in the Panel’s testimony are presented as FY costs, as reflected in 
17 
Exhibit __ (GIOP-1).  To present the Rate Year and Data Year revenue 
18 
requirements reflected in this case, the Company used the FY capital plan 
19 
forecasts and cash flowed them based on historic spending patterns to develop 
20

rate year schedules presented in the exhibits of the Revenue Requirements 
1 
Panel.   
2 
 
3 
           The Company’s projected O&M expenditures have also been converted to Rate 
4 
Year and Data Year amounts and are represented that way in relevant exhibits 
5 
and the revenue requirement. 
6

Q. 
How do the Company’s capital investments and programs align with the 
14 
goals of the Climate Leadership and Community Protection Act 
15 
(“CLCPA”)? 
16 
A. 
As discussed more in the direct testimony of the Company’s Future of Heat 
17 
Panel, the Company’s gas networks will play an integral role in meeting the 
18 
State’s ambitious climate goals and delivering the low-carbon economy of the 
19 
future.  To that end, the Company is advancing various proposals to support the 
20 
2050 net-zero emission goal and 85 percent emission reduction target 
21 


--- Page 15 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 13 of 105 
 
 
 
recognizing that its gas system will play an integral role in meeting these 
1 
ambitious targets and delivering the low-carbon economy of the future.  These 
2 
include: (i) reducing emissions resulting from customer energy use, (ii) 
3 
promoting gas demand response and other non-pipeline alternatives (“NPAs”); 
4 
(iii) encouraging the development of sustainable heating options; and (iv) 
5 
developing new technologies to advance the low carbon heating solutions 
6 
needed for the future.  In this testimony, the Panel discusses the significant 
7 
efforts to reduce methane emissions from the gas distribution system. 
8 
 
9 
Q.      Does the Company anticipate any long-term impacts from the COVID-19 
10 
pandemic on its investment plans? 
11 
A.       Local economies in areas served by the Company experienced significant 
12 
immediate impacts following implementation of the public health measures.  
13 
With many companies reducing or suspending operations since the start of the 
14 
lockdown, and many more people being home during the traditional work week, 
15 
the Company has seen less overall demand and also shifts in load (i.e., increased 
16 
residential load and decreased commercial and industrial demand during the 
17 
week).  Although the pandemic has impacted customers and the Company in 
18 
the short-term, the long-term need for investment in the gas system continues. 
19 
 
20 


--- Page 16 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 14 of 105 
 
 
 
Prior to this filing, the Company reviewed its gas capital plan, previously filed 
1 
in Case 17-G-0239 on March 31, 2020, and made certain adjustments to 
2 
investment levels in light of recent events, including, but not limited to, the 
3 
COVID-19 pandemic, which will mitigate customer bill impacts during the 
4 
term of this rate plan.  The Company will continue to evaluate system and 
5 
customer needs and adjust the capital plan, as necessary; however, it does not 
6 
anticipate that the COVID-19 pandemic will result in significant long-lasting 
7 
impacts on the capital investment or O&M plans.  
8 
 
9 
III. 
Capital and O&M Plan Objectives and Priorities 
10 
A. Plan Objectives  
11 
Q. 
Please describe the overall objective of the Company’s infrastructure and 
12 
operations plans. 
13 
A. 
The Company’s gas infrastructure and operations plans are designed to provide 
14 
safe and reliable gas delivery service to customers at reasonable costs.  As 
15 
shown on Exhibit __ (GIOP-2), over the last several years, the Company has 
16 
increased investment to modernize and enhance the resiliency of its gas assets.  
17 
 
18 
Significant capital investment over the next several years is required to ensure 
19 
that the gas system continues to operate safely while meeting the demands of 
20 
customers and to enable further modernization of the Company’s facilities and 
21 


--- Page 17 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 15 of 105 
 
 
 
operations in a manner that achieves meaningful methane emissions reductions.  
1 
The proposed plan includes capital and O&M spending to meet both customer 
2 
needs and satisfy state and federal regulatory requirements and goals, including 
3 
retirement of LPP, gas pipeline safety enhancements, and deployment of NPAs.  
4 
While significant investments are needed to meet these needs and objectives, in 
5 
developing its capital and O&M plans, the Company balanced the need for 
6 
spending to achieve safety and service objectives with the need to manage costs 
7 
and minimize impacts on customer rates.     
8 
 
9 
Q. 
What are the Company’s projected direct capital forecasts? 
10 
A. 
A summary of the Company’s total direct capital plan forecast for the Rate Year 
11 
and Data Years is provided in Table 1. 
12

15 
Q. 
Why are the Company’s capital investment needs increasing? 
16 
A. 
Several developments have required Niagara Mohawk and other natural gas 
17 
distribution utilities to increase their annual capital expenditures.  Notably, 
18 
pipeline safety incidents, such as the tragic events in San Bruno, California, 
19 
Allentown, Pennsylvania, and more recent incidents, including East Harlem, 
20 
New York and Merrimack Valley, Massachusetts, have appropriately increased 
21 


--- Page 18 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 16 of 105 
 
 
 
focus on pipeline safety and the need to carefully monitor and replace aging 
1 
pipeline infrastructure.  Weather-related events such as Superstorm Sandy, 
2 
Hurricanes Irene and Lee, and the Polar Vortex, and the expectation that similar 
3 
events will continue to occur, require the Company to find ways to protect its 
4 
facilities from severe weather.   
5 
 
6 
In response to these events, the industry has adopted new safety requirements 
7 
and is implementing best practices to modernize and protect gas facilities.  For 
8 
example, since the 2017 Rate Case, new safety regulations require additional 
9 
safety inspections of inside service line and plastic fusions.  PHMSA has issued 
10 
new safety requirements related to transmission system integrity management 
11 
and verification; and the New York State Legislature is currently considering a 
12 
bill that would require licensed professional engineers (“PEs”) to review and 
13 
certify gas facility design plans.  The Commission and utility industry review 
14 
of recent incidents has also resulted in recommendations for safety best 
15 
practices, including overpressure protection improvements and enhanced 
16 
contractor oversight.  These required and recommended improvements 
17 
necessitate significant additional investments and compliance resources. 
18 
 
19 
Additionally, the costs of delivering mandated programs have recently 
20 
increased for a variety of reasons, including increased paving requirements as 
21 


--- Page 19 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 17 of 105 
 
 
 
work is performed in more urban areas.  These new mandates and increasing 
1 
maintenance and construction costs are drivers of the proposed increases to 
2 
capital investments and O&M programs over the next several years.  The factors 
3 
contributing to increased costs and the Company’s plan to mitigate rising costs 
4 
are presented in more detail below. 
5 
 
6 
Q. 
Why are increases in capital expenditures necessary over the next several 
7 
years?   
8 
A. 
The same factors that lead to increased capital spending over the last several 
9 
years will require a continued commitment to modernize the Company’s gas 
10 
transmission and distribution assets, increase the size and scope of its safety, 
11 
LPP retirement, and reliability programs, and support customer needs.   
12 
 
13 
Q. 
How will the Company support this increased level of capital investment? 
14 
A. 
As the Company developed plans to modernize its gas assets, it also began to 
15 
build and enhance its operations, engineering, resource planning, work 
16 
management, and quality control organizations and capabilities to deliver 
17 
increasing levels of capital investment.  The Company will continue to develop 
18 
these capabilities in the Rate Year and Data Years by adding incremental labor 
19 
resources to execute the capital plan and support the increased operations 
20 
workload (discussed in Section V).  The capital investment plan includes 
21 


--- Page 20 ---

6

i. 
Proactive Main and Service Replacement (LPP) Program  
1 
Q. 
Please describe the inventory of LPP existing on the Company’s system.   
2 
A. 
LPP in Niagara Mohawk’s service territory is defined as pipe less than 16 inches 
3 
in diameter that is: (i) unprotected (i.e., non-cathodically protected) steel pipe 
4 
(whether bare or coated), (ii) cast and wrought iron pipe, (iii) pre-1985 vintage 
5 
Aldyl-A plastic pipe, and (iv) unprotected steel/wrought iron, copper, pre-1974 
6 
High Density Polyethylene (“HDPE”) and Aldyl-A services (“associated 
7 
services”).  As of the end of CY 2019, the Company has approximately 514 
8 
miles of LPP in its remaining inventory.  As would be expected, the Company 
9 
has observed a significantly higher leak rate on its LPP inventory as compared 
10 
to all other distribution facilities.  While the current LPP inventory represents 
11 
only six percent of Niagara Mohawk’s distribution system, LPP is responsible 
12 
for 90 percent of leak repairs, excluding excavation damages.   
13 
 
14 
Q. 
What is the Company’s proposal regarding its Proactive Main and Service 
15 
(LPP) Replacement Program? 
16 
A. 
To reduce the risk of leaks and breaks, improve system performance and 
17 
reliability, meet the Company’s commitment to enhance customer satisfaction 
18 
and reduce methane emissions, the Company has prioritized the retirement of 
19 
higher-risk gas infrastructure – specifically, LPP and associated services – that 
20 
disproportionally contributes to leaks on Niagara Mohawk’s system.   
21 


--- Page 26 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 24 of 105 
 
 
 
 
1 
The existing plan, approved by the Commission in the 2017 Rate Case, requires 
2 
that the Company retire a cumulative total of at least 150 total miles of LPP by 
3 
the end of CY 2020.  The Company’s proposal is to continue retirement of 
4 
approximately 50 miles of LPP per year on average for the term of the rate plan.  
5 
The metrics and targets for LPP replacements are presented in detail in the 
6 
Company’s Gas Safety Panel testimony.  Under the Company’s proposal, all 
7 
LPP will be eliminated by CY 2034, ahead of the Commission’s stated policy 
8 
goal of full LPP retirement by CY 2035 (Case 15-G-0151). 
9 
 
10 
Q. 
Why is the Company proposing to continue the current pace of LPP 
11 
retirement? 
12 
A. 
As the Company has worked over the last several years to retire LPP, the 
13 
Company has observed a leveling-off of the overall leak rate, as shown in Table 
14 
2.  While leak rates on LPP still outpace the leak rate for overall distribution 
15 
piping by more than ten times and witnessed an overall increase in the past few 
16 
years, the rise in leak repairs of LPP is largely driven by the Company’s 
17 
commitment to decrease the overall leak backlog and cold weather has resulted 
18 
in cast iron joint leaks.  The Company believes this increase is temporary and 
19 
continued replacement at the same pace will decrease the leak rate and optimize 
20 
the resources.  The current leak rate for all distribution piping is 0.06 leaks per 
21 


--- Page 27 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 25 of 105 
 
 
 
mile, excluding damages from excavation.  The 2018 leak rate for LPP was 0.87 
1 
leaks per mile.  
2 
 
3 
Table 2:  Niagara Mohawk Historic Leak Repair Rate for Mains 
4 
 
5 
The Company’s proposal is designed to lower the overall cost of the LPP 
6 
retirement program in the near term while also allowing for early completion of 
7 
the state’s policy goals for LPP retirement.  Exhibit __ (GIOP-3) provides 
8 
projected leak rates for LPP for various main replacement strategies. 
9 
 
10 
Q. 
Is there an environmental benefit associated with the retirement of LPP? 
11 
A. 
Yes.  Retirement of LPP reduces gas losses and fugitive emissions of methane.  
12 
As discussed in the direct testimony of the Company’s Future of Heat Panel, 
13

throughout its U.S. service territories, National Grid has proposed an aggressive 
1

2 
a high-level estimate of potential methane emissions reductions over the next 
3 
several years assuming the retirement of LPP pursuant to Niagara Mohawk’s 
4 
proposed program.

9 
Additionally, the Company’s LPP retirement algorithm prioritizes retirement of 
10

Q. 
Which programs contribute to retirement of LPP to meet the planned 
4 
targets?  
 
5 
A. 
A small percentage of LPP is retired through the Public Works and Gas System 
6 
Reliability Programs.  The bulk of LPP retirement, however, occurs through the 
7 
Company’s Proactive Main Replacement Program.  The Company forecasts 
8 
that the Proactive Main Replacement Program will replace approximately 41 
9 
miles in FY 2022 and approximately 46 miles per year in FYs 2023-2025.  The 
10 
Proactive Main Replacement Program also includes retirement of the associated 
11 
services.   
12 
 
13 
Q. 
How does the Company prioritize the retirement of main segments for the 
14 
Proactive Main and Service Replacement Program? 
15 
A. 
Each year, the Company prioritizes retirement of LPP segments by using a risk 
16 
ranking algorithm that is part of the Company’s Distribution Integrity 
17 
Management Plan (“DIMP”) and the Company’s Gas Operating Procedure for 
18 
the Identification, Evaluation and Prioritization of Distribution Main Segments 
19 
for Replacement (ENG04030).  The Company’s risk model calculates a relative 
20 
risk score for each LPP segment based on specific performance data and 
21 


--- Page 30 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 28 of 105 
 
 
 
localized incident probabilities and consequences, combined with calculated 
1 
risk factors for the asset classes being evaluated.  This risk-based algorithm, 
2 
along with the Company’s good engineering judgment, which takes all factors 
3 
and risks into consideration in each case, form the foundation of the LPP 
4 
retirement strategy.  The Company is proposing to target more cast iron main 
5 
for retirement where possible in consideration of other risk factors, due to 
6 
increased leaks and risk of this material.   
7 
 
8 
Q. 
Does the Company’s Proactive Main and Service Replacement Program 
9 
include projects to address low system pressure resulting from LPP 
10 
retirements? 
11 
A. 
Yes.  Beginning in the Rate Year, this program includes main work, such as 
12 
installation of new main or replacement of non-leak prone distribution main, 
13 
that is necessary to retire LPP.  More than half of the remaining LPP inventory 
14 
is located on the low-pressure system, and LPP is often either a main feed to an 
15 
area or is essential to maintaining minimum pressures on connected facilities.  
16 
As the remaining LPP is retired or upgraded to medium pressure, low pressure 
17 
feeds may be eliminated creating low pressure zones on non-leak prone 
18 
distribution main.  The Company estimates that two miles per year of new main 
19 
installation or non-leak prone main replacement may be necessary to enable 
20 
LPP retirement.  
21

ii. 
Integrity Management and Integrity Verification Programs 
11 
Q. 
What is the Company’s Integrity Management Program (“IMP”)? 
12 
A. 
The Company’s transmission pipeline IMP is a safety program mandated by the 
13 
Pipeline Safety Improvement Act of 2002 and corresponding DOT regulations.  
14 
The IMP identifies and addresses potential issues affecting the physical 
15 
soundness of Company facilities before they become safety or performance 
16 
issues.  The Company conducts baseline and periodic reassessments of 
17 
transmission facilities to identify and evaluate potential threats to “Covered 
18 
Segments” of pipelines, i.e., transmission pipelines that could affect High 
19 
Consequence Areas (areas where a pipeline failure could have significant 
20 


--- Page 35 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 33 of 105 
 
 
 
adverse consequences), as well as remediation of significant defects discovered 
1 
during such assessments. 
2 
 
3 
Niagara Mohawk’s IMP is designed to proactively address system risk 
4 
identified through integrity assessments in a manner that will also enable the 
5 
Company to cost effectively comply with existing and new IMP regulations.   
6 
 
7 
Q. 
Please describe the IMP capital investments.  
8 
A. 
Currently, IMP assessments may be accomplished through either external 
9 
corrosion direct assessment (“ECDA”) or in-line inspection (“ILI”) technology, 
10 
which uses a device known as a “pig” to evaluate the condition of the outside 
11 
and inside of the pipeline.  ILI is considered to be superior to ECDA because 
12 
of the ability to identify condition problems originating internally or during 
13 
manufacture of the pipe.  IMP investments are necessary to: (i) support in-line 
14 
inspections (e.g., installation of pig launchers and receivers, and pipe 
15 
reconfiguration/replacement), and (ii) to resolve issues discovered during 
16 
pipeline inspections conducted through either method.   
17 
 
18 
Q. 
Please describe the IMP capital investment needs for the Rate Year and 
19 
Data Years. 
20 


--- Page 36 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 34 of 105 
 
 
 
Currently, 49 percent of the Company’s DOT transmission pipeline is ILI 
1 
enabled, increased from 17 percent when the 2017 Rate Case was filed.  The 
2 
Company’s capital plan will ultimately result in 94 percent of the Company’s 
3 
DOT pipeline being ILI enabled.  This will significantly improve the 
4 
Company’s ability to identify integrity issues.   
5 
 
6 
Q. 
What is the status of federal regulations in this area?   
7 
A. 
In May of 2016, PHMSA issued a Notice of Proposed Rulemaking (“NPRM”) 
8 
that proposes new pipeline safety regulations that include a requirement for 
9 
increased inspection of IMP-covered pipelines utilizing ILI technology and an 
10 
expanded definition of Medium Consequence Areas (“MCAs”), as well as a 
11 
host of other requirements.  To meet these requirements, transmission pipelines 
12 
must be ILI enabled, where possible.  The NPRM was adopted in a final 
13 
rulemaking on October 1, 2019 (“Final Rulemaking”), with an effective date of 
14 
July 1, 2020.  Because the Company’s IMP workplan is risk based, and in light 
15 
of PHMSA’s new regulations expanding IMP, the Company believes that its 
16 
proposed IMP program is a reasonable and conservative approach to managing 
17 
pipeline integrity during the Rate Year and Data Years. 
18 
 
19 
Q. 
How are the IMP workplan and associated IMP capital investment forecast 
20 
determined?  
21 


--- Page 37 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 35 of 105 
 
 
 
A. 
The workplan began in 2002 based on initial assessments using the Company’s 
1 
risk model.  Segments were prioritized for ILI enablement over a multi-year 
2 
workplan based on a combination of their risk score and other relevant factors, 
3 
such as facility characteristics or geography, to determine what work to do 
4 
when.  Segments must be re-assessed at a maximum of seven years according 
5 
to the date of last assessment.  The workplan is updated annually and as required 
6 
assessments are completed.  Additionally, ILI projects typically consist of three 
7 
years of design and procurement work followed by construction in the fourth 
8 
year.   
9 
 
10 
IMP spending can fluctuate significantly year to year depending on the projects 
11 
identified for inclusion in the workplan.  Because the work must be done by 
12 
segment, the length, geographic location, and characteristics of the segments 
13 
that need to be addressed each year contribute to different costs such that 
14 
historic program costs are not indicative of future program needs.  Once 
15 
segments that will be addressed by the workplan are identified, cost forecasts, 
16 
known as level one project estimates, are developed based on prior projects of 
17 
similar size and scope adjusted for known variables.  Additionally, some larger 
18 
projects in the IMP are separately budgeted on the capital investment plan.       
19 
 
20 


--- Page 38 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 36 of 105 
 
 
 
The IMP workplan  is described in more detail in the IMP data sheet in Exhibit 
1 
__ (GIOP-4).  The IMP capital investments required to address known risks 
2 
identified via assessments and to increase ILI enablement are approximately 
3 
$26.975 million in FY 2022, $14.657 million in FY 2023, $28.005 million in 
4 
FY 2024, and $20.930 million in FY 2025.  The operating expenditures required 
5 
for the IMP workplan are approximately $3.209 million in the Rate Year, 
6 
$1.402 million in Data Year 1, and $1.499 million in Data Year 2. 
7 
 
8 
Q. 
What is covered in the Company’s IVP Program? 
9 
A. 
The Pipeline Safety Act of 2011 mandates that PHMSA establish rules 
10 
requiring operators to demonstrate their pipelines are “fit for service” by 
11 
reviewing construction records for each pipeline segment to confirm it is 
12 
operating within design parameters.  The Final Rulemaking establishes new 
13 
requirements regarding the maximum allowable operating pressure (“MAOP”) 
14 
and pressure testing requirements for existing pipelines, including (i) 
15 
eliminating the exemption for establishing the MAOP of pre-1970 
16 
“grandfathered” pipe segments; (ii) mandating additional pressure testing or 
17 
replacement for pipelines without adequate pressure test records; and (iii) 
18 
requiring operators who lack certain records to establish material properties 
19 
using approved methods (e.g., cutting and testing pipe samples).  In advance of 
20 
a final rulemaking, PHMSA issued an advisory bulletin (ADB-11-01) directing 
21 


--- Page 39 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 37 of 105 
 
 
 
operators to perform a detailed threat and risk analysis that includes a 
1 
comprehensive records review of their systems.   
2 
 
3 
The Company’s IVP program began in 2011 and includes thorough record 
4 
reviews, pipeline replacement, and retirement of non-essential pipeline 
5 
segments.  As with the IMP, the IVP is based on the Company’s assessment of 
6 
system risks, while also incorporating PHMSA’s rulemaking.   
7 
 
8 
Q. 
What is the status of the Company’s IVP records review and its IVP 
9 
Program proposal for the Rate Year and Data Years? 
10 
A. 
Through its IVP to date, Niagara Mohawk has completed the MAOP records 
11 
review on 100 percent of its DOT-jurisdictional pipelines.  Going forward, the 
12 
IVP addresses transmission main replacements and testing necessitated by 
13 
incomplete records identified by the review and pressure testing and preparing 
14 
to comply with the additional testing required by the new rules.  Where the 
15 
Company has identified incomplete records, pipelines will be replaced, or 
16 
records will be recreated through testing. 
17 
 
18 
The Company’s proposed IVP work plan incorporates elements of the Final 
19 
Rulemaking and PHMSA guidance document ADB-11-01 as well as conduct 
20 
records review, replace pipeline segments, retire non-essential pipeline 
21 


--- Page 40 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 38 of 105 
 
 
 
segments, and perform critical engineering assessments.  The IVP work plan is 
1 
described in more detail in the IVP data sheet in Exhibit __ (GIOP-4) and is 
2 
approximately $1.407 million in FY 2022, $9.155 million in FY 2023, and 
3 
$8.425 million in FY 2024.  The operating expenditures required for the IVP

5

7 
iii. 
Transmission Station Integrity Program 
8 
Q. 
Please describe the Transmission Station Integrity Program. 
9 
A. 
This program is similar to the Company’s IVP, but the program addresses 
10 
transmission-connected pressure regulating stations and gate stations 
11 
(“transmission stations”).  The Final Rulemaking requires that operators: 
12 
• Confirm the records used to justify MAOP (TVC); 
13 
• Reconfirm station MAOPs using assessment methods; 
14 
• Perform routine assessments on station piping in both high consequence 
15 
and medium consequence areas; 
16 
• Consider seismicity as a risk factor in IM; 
17 
• Perform material sampling to establish properties; and 
18 
• Advance fitness for service analysis. 
19 
Related advisory bulletins and discussions between PHMSA and industry 
20 
stakeholders during the NPRM and Final Rulemaking process have indicated 
21 


--- Page 41 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 39 of 105 
 
 
 
that the proposed rules apply to transmission stations.  Therefore, the Company 
1 
is proposing a program for IVP records review and, where records are 
2 
inadequate to demonstrate that transmission station facilities are fit for service, 
3 
capital investments for station rehabilitation, or partial or full station 
4 
replacements to bring the stations into compliance.  Similar to the IMP and IVP 
5 
Programs for transmission pipelines, these investments are prudent and in 
6 
compliance with the final rulemaking to address risk presented by older 
7 
pressure regulating facilities for which there are inadequate records. 
8 
 
9 
 
The Company began ramping up this program in FY 2020.  The program will 
10 
reach full implementation in FY 2023 and spending will levelize year over year 
11 
after FY 2023.  The program forecast is based on investment grade estimates to 
12 
address the population of existing transmission stations that will be covered by 
13 
the program.  The capital investments for this program are $3.007 million in the 
14 
FY 2022, $12.928 million in FY 2023, $13.187 million in FY 2024, and 
15 
$13.451 million in FY 2025. 
16

iv. 
Pipeline Replacement Projects 
18 
Q. 
Please describe the Pipeline 16 Segment Replacement Project. 
19 
A. 
Pipeline 16 (“PL 16”) is a 34.7 mile-long pipeline running through the 
20 
Company’s service territory with a MAOP of 473 psig.  PL 16’s current class 
21 


--- Page 42 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 40 of 105 
 
 
 
location was based on the population at the time of the pipeline’s construction 
1 
in 1958.  Recent population increases in areas served by the pipeline have 
2 
resulted in a change to the class location and require that either PL 16’s MAOP 
3 
is reduced or PL 16 is replaced and tested with new pipe that meets the new 
4 
class location design requirements. 
5 
 
6 
This project will replace approximately seven miles (37,000 feet) of DOT 
7 
classified transmission pipeline with new pressure tested 24” pipe that meets 
8 
the current class location in accordance with 16 NYCRR Part 255.611.  Pipeline 
9 
replacement will occur in three areas of the Company’s Central division – 
10 
Chittenango, Cicero, and Clay.   
11 
 
12 
Q. 
What are the benefits of this replacement program? 
13 
A. 
The original pressure test for PL 16 is no longer appropriate for the current class 
14 
location.  Replacing existing pipeline segments with piping that meets current 
15 
class location requirements will bring PL 16 into compliance with applicable 
16 
regulations, respond to current system requirements, and enhance the safety and 
17 
reliability of the pipeline. 
18 
 
19 
Q. 
What is the timeline and capital forecast for this program? 
20

3 
in the FY 2022, $6.500 million in FY 2023, $26.000 million in FY 2024, and 
4 
$76.100 million in FY 2025.

v. 
Professional Engineer (“PE”) Stamping Program 
11 
Q. 
Please describe the Company’s proposal to add professional engineering 
12 
review and approval of engineering plans for gas system work. 
13 
A. 
In response to a 2018 gas safety incident involving Columbia Gas, the National 
14 
Transportation Safety Board issued certain safety recommendations for gas 
15 
utilities.  One of the safety recommendations suggested that licensed PEs should 
16 
review and approve all gas engineering plans through a process known as 
17 
stamping.  Massachusetts and Rhode Island have recently passed legislation 
18 
requiring PE stamping of gas design work.  Similar legislation has not been 
19 
passed in New York; however, the Company anticipates that similar legislation 
20 


--- Page 45 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 43 of 105 
 
 
 
will be adopted in New York at some point during the Rate Year and Data 
1 
Years.   
2 
 
3 
Q. 
What level of capital investment will be required in implement a new PE 
4 
Stamping Program? 
5 
A. 
The cost of PE stamping in FY 2023 is approximately $3.950 million across all 
6 
capital categories, and the cost will remain consistent, in FY 2024-FY 2025 
7 
subject to inflation.  The cost will be allocated based on the guidance currently 
8 
being used in both Massachusetts and Rhode Island for the type of work that 
9 
requires a PE stamp and the projected volumes of work associated with that 
10 
guidance from the capital investment plan. 
11

D. Gas Cost Estimating Performance Metric 
13 
Q. 
Please explain the Company’s current gas cost estimating performance 
14 
metric. 
15 
A. 
In the 2017 Joint Proposal, the Company agreed to a fiscal year performance 
16 
metric for certain complex gas capital projects.  The metric tracks the variance 
17 
between the Company’s project estimate and the actual spend to complete the 
18 
project.  The Company is subject to a fixed negative revenue adjustment if the 
19 
total percentage of projects with variances greater than ten percent reaches 
20 
specified levels of twenty, thirty, or forty percent.  The Company annually 
21 


--- Page 46 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 44 of 105 
 
 
 
reports its performance on this metric to the Commission.  To the Company’s 
1 
knowledge, Niagara Mohawk is the only utility in New York subject to this 
2 
metric. 
3 
 
4 
Q. 
How has the Company historically performed on this metric? 
5 
A. 
The Company exceeded the performance target for this metric in each year 
6 
since its adoption and has not incurred any associated negative revenue 
7 
adjustments. 
8 
 
9 
Q. 
Does the Company propose any changes to the metric? 
10 
A. 
Yes.  The Company proposes to eliminate the metric as the Company has 
11 
improved upon its complex capital estimating practices and performance over 
12 
the past several years.  Specifically, the Company has: 
13 
• Merged the Gas and Electric Estimating Departments under a single 
14 
Director, 
driving 
consistency, 
efficiency, 
and 
best 
practice 
15 
implementation; 
16 
• Established the Network Development Process, under the direction of 
17 
the Complex Capital Delivery Organization, which provides a Network 
18 
Development Playbook to provide instructions on the management of 
19 
complex capital projects; 
20 


--- Page 47 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 45 of 105 
 
 
 
• Implemented the new risk process and Risk Playbook to quantify, 
1 
define, and add transparency to the risk contingency dollars in the 
2 
Company’s published estimates using a Risk Register and Monte-Carlo 
3 
simulations;  
4 
• Launched an industry leading estimating software, known as SAGE 
5 
Estimating, along with industry accepted utility estimating knowledge 
6 
bases with additional estimating assemblies and functionalities to 
7 
replace outdated excel based internal estimating software tools; and  
8 
• Changed its estimating reporting to align with industry standards using 
9 
a three-point estimating reporting method (Base, P50 & P80 Values). 
10 
 
11 
The Company believes that the changes noted above have addressed many, if 
12 
not all, of the Commission’s concerns regarding complex capital estimating 
13 
accuracy and has improved the Company’s overall complex capital estimating 
14 
performance.  Additionally, the capital reports filed with the Commission 
15 
provide project cost variance information and will continue to give the 
16 
Commission the ability to monitor the Company’s project estimates and 
17 
performance against its capital plan.  Therefore, the Company believes that 
18 
continuation of this metric to measure its performance during the Rate Year and 
19 
Data Years is no longer necessary.

5

Q. 
Please describe some of the technologies and practices the Company uses 
14

15

The Company continues to employ a number of technologies and best practices 
16 
designed to increase the efficiency and reduce the cost of its capital 
17 
expenditures.  These practices include: 
18 
• Increasing the amount of planned capital work (versus reactive work). 
19 


--- Page 52 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 50 of 105 
 
 
 
• Increasing coordination among capital programs to increase efficiencies 
1 
(e.g., leveraging LPP opportunities).

• Installing more small-diameter, high-pressure facilities that can be 
3

4

5 
and reducing instances of damage (because of decreased size and weight 
6 
of equipment).

9

10 
• Continuing to deploy cured-in-place (“CIP”) lining (as described in 
11 
more detail below). 
12 
In addition, as noted above, the Company’s Capital Delivery Group has 
13 
identified opportunities for efficiencies that have been used to reduce the 
14 
Company’s projected capital costs in FY 2022 – FY 2025.

16 
A. Capital Planning, Budgeting and Sanctioning Process 
17 
Q. 
Please describe the annual development of the Company’s capital plan. 
18 
A. 
Each year, the Company develops a ten-year gas capital plan to achieve its 
19 
performance objectives of delivering safe and reliable service.  Investment 
20 


--- Page 53 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 51 of 105 
 
 
 
Planning compiles proposed spending for programs and individual capital 
1 
projects.  Programs and projects are categorized into one of four spending 
2 
rationales (Mandated, Customer Connections, Reliability and Non-
3 
Infrastructure).   
4 
 
5 
The Company currently uses a tool, C55 (known internally as “Copperleaf”), 
6 
for the prioritization and optimization of capital investments through a risk 
7 
scoring and prioritization methodology.  This tool uses criteria aligned to a 
8 
common scale to compare dissimilar inputs (e.g., financial and non-financial 
9 
inputs) to determine the set of investments, alternatives, and the timing of 
10 
projects that have the highest potential value.  
11 
 
12 
After the Company ranks the investments in C55, meetings are scheduled with 
13 
budget sponsors to review value models, project rankings, and various scenarios 
14 
produced through the optimization of investments to meet capital planning 
15 
targets.  Once plan targets have been approved by the Gas Business 
16 
Organization, they are submitted to the Finance organization for review. 
17 
 
18 
In late fall, the capital plan is reviewed by the New York Jurisdictional 
19 
President and the Finance organization.  The New York Jurisdictional President 
20 
reviews the overall customer, service quality and financial impacts of the 
21 


--- Page 54 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 52 of 105 
 
 
 
investment plan as part of the business planning process and may request 
1 
changes to the level or mix of investments.   
2 
 
3 
In early winter, the capital plan is presented to the NGUSA Board of Directors 
4 
and the National Grid plc Executive Committee and, in early spring, the capital 
5 
portfolio is presented to the National Grid plc Board of Directors for review and 
6 
approval.   
7 
 
8 
Q.     Are there additional approvals needed before a project in the annual 
9 
capital plan may proceed? 
10 
A.     Yes.  Aside from the capital planning and budgeting process, specific delegation 
11 
of authority (“DOA”) approval must be obtained for any project in the ten-year 
12 
capital plan to proceed.  This process includes the sanctioning documentation 
13 
and review for projects over $1 million and other levels of review for smaller 
14 
projects.  Presently, all projects greater than $1 million require some level of 
15 
sanctioning documentation and review.  The U.S. Sanctioning Committee 
16 
(“USSC”) was established by the National Grid USA Board of Directors 
17 
specifically for this purpose.  For projects between $1 million and $8 million, 
18 
the USSC has delegated review to an informal committee led by the investment 
19 
planning group and including, but not limited to, managers and directors from 
20 
the regulatory, estimating, asset management, and procurement groups.  The 
21 


--- Page 55 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 53 of 105 
 
 
 
committee reviews and finalizes sanctioning papers for these projects at a 
1 
weekly meeting, and the committee then forwards the final sanction documents 
2 
to the executive sponsor of the project for approval and signature.  Projects 
3 
between $8 million and $25 million are reviewed and approved by the USSC.  
4 
Projects between $25 million to $203 million are reviewed by the USSC and 
5 
then are forwarded to a Senior Executive Sanctioning Committee (“SESC”) for 
6 
review and approval.  Projects less than $1 million do not require sanctioning 
7 
and are approved through a supervisory delegation of authority hierarchy based 
8 
on certain established thresholds.   
9 
 
10 
Q.    
Please explain the difference between the DOA review and approval 
11 
(sanctioning) process and the approved five-year capital plan used to 
12 
forecast FY 2021 – FY 2025. 
13 
A.      The timing of the sanctioning process is not aligned with the capital planning 
14 
process used to forecast FY 2022 – FY 2025 for purposes of this rate filing.  As 
15 
described above, the Company develops a long-term investment plan that is 
16 
used as the basis for the forecast for the rate case proposal.  Project sanctioning, 
17 
however, normally occurs immediately prior to the fiscal year for which the 
18 
investment is planned.  For example, the FY 2022 capital plan will be formally 
19 
sanctioned in early 2021.  Thus, the Company’s currently-sanctioned or 
20 


--- Page 56 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 54 of 105 
 
 
 
partially-sanctioned projects do not yet represent the full capital forecast 
1 
proposed in this rate case proposal. 
2 
 
3 
Q. 
Please describe how the Company’s DIMP impacts its capital investment 
4 
planning. 
5 
A. 
The DIMP involves a risk-based assessment of the Company’s distribution 
6 
system to identify threats in seven categories: corrosion, natural forces, 
7 
excavation damage, other outside force damage, material and weld failure, 
8 
equipment failure/malfunction and inappropriate operation.  The DIMP 
9 
requires evaluation and prioritization of the risks that these threats pose, and the 
10 
implementation of measures to address the highest risks with an emphasis on 
11 
leak management, enhanced damage prevention, operator qualification to 
12 
reduce human error and system replacement.  Consistent with the DIMP, the 
13 
Company prioritizes asset replacements in its investment plan based on a risk 
14 
ranking that considers, among other things, leak repair history, types of leak, 
15 
pipe material, surrounding geography, segment length, nearby construction 
16 
activity, field conditions, customer issues, open leaks and engineering 
17 
judgment.  The risk ranking factors are carefully designed to consider known 
18 
differences in the performance of asset subclasses, extensive experience with 
19 
asset failures, current performance data for the asset subclasses for various 
20 


--- Page 57 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 55 of 105 
 
 
 
threat categories, and subject matter experts’ analysis and opinions on the future 
1 
performance of the assets. 
2 
 
3 
B. Customer Connections Category of Capital Spending 
4 
Q. 
Please describe what is included in the Customer Connections category. 
5 
A. 
The Customer Connections category of work accounts for approximately 16.41 
6 
percent ($33.878 million) of the total planned capital investment in FY 2022. 
7 
 
8 
Niagara Mohawk is required by the Public Service Law, the Commission’s 
9 
regulations, and its tariff to extend service to new customers in its service 
10 
territory in proximity to the Company’s facilities, and to install new distribution 
11 
main, up to a 100-foot tariff allowance per customer, to do so.  Expanding the 
12 
availability of natural gas in Niagara Mohawk’s service territory can bring 
13 
significant economic benefits in the form of energy cost savings for customers, 
14 
job creation, and increased local tax revenues.  Moreover, there are 
15 
environmental benefits associated with conversion to natural gas from other, 
16 
higher emission fuels such as fuel oil.  To enable new customer connections, 
17 
the Company must make significant capital investments in mains, services, and 
18 
system reinforcements.  Customer Connection programs are designed to 
19 
support forecast customer demand by increasing system utilization in a cost-
20 


--- Page 58 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 56 of 105 
 
 
 
effective way.  Customer Connections programs involve the installation of new 
1 
mains, services and meters and include system reinforcement.   
2 
 
3 
The Customer Connections – Install Main, Install Services, Meter Purchases, 
4 
and Install Meter Programs and costs are presented in Exhibit __ (GIOP-4).

Q. 
Please describe recent customer trends in the Company’s service territory. 
7 
A. 
The economic outlook in the near-term will be severely impacted by the 
8 
COVID-19 pandemic.  However, as the New York economy re-opens, the 
9 
current economic forecasts predict a rebound during the term of the proposed 
10 
rate plan.  Prior to the pandemic, growth trends showed an increase in the 
11 
multifamily sector.  Steady demand in the residential conversion market existed 
12 
due to low natural gas prices compared to oil and propane while commercial 
13 
conversion market is expected to decrease slightly due to market saturation and 
14 
fewer large project opportunities.  Overall, the demand for gas has increased 
15 
and is expected to continue through 2025 due to the gas price advantage.  New 
16 
construction in the residential and multi-family markets also drove increases in 
17 
growth.

Given the pre-pandemic growth trends, and the expectation that the economy 
1 
will recover during the rate plan period, the Company expects that customer 
2 
requests will increase as the state reopens through each stage.

4 
Q. 
Please describe the Gas System Reinforcement Program in the Customer 
5 
Connections category of spending. 
6 
A. 
The Gas System Reinforcement Program contains projects intended to ensure 
7 
that minimum system pressures are maintained throughout the gas network 
8 
during periods of peak demand.  The Company models peak demand based on 
9 
the send-out forecasts developed by the Advanced Data Analytics group 
10 
(Company Witness Theodore E. Poe).  As a result of increased demand for gas 
11 
usage in its service territory, Niagara Mohawk has determined that it is 
12 
necessary to complete a number of projects to ensure it retains its ability to meet 
13 
peak requirements.  These reinforcement projects are essential to serve growing 
14 
demand and to maintain reliable service to existing customers.   
15 
 
16 
During the winter of 2015/2016, Niagara Mohawk recorded two of its top ten 
17 
sendout records, including the second highest firm load record of 997,343 
18 
dekatherms on February 14, 2016, when the average of Syracuse and Albany 
19 
daily temperatures was minus 2 degrees Fahrenheit, and the third highest firm 
20 
load record of 987,172 dekatherms on February 13, 2016, when the average 
21 


--- Page 60 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 58 of 105 
 
 
 
daily temperature was minus 7.5 degrees Fahrenheit.  January 6, 2018 and 
1 
January 21, 2019 also made it to the top ten sendout list at the fifth and seventh 
2 
position.  The sendout on these days were 970,290 dekatherms (minus 1 degree 
3 
Fahrenheit average temperature) and 960,837 dekatherms (3 degrees Fahrenheit 
4 
average temperature), respectively.  The recent growth in peak sendout 
5 
underscores the need to ensure that minimum system design pressures are 
6 
maintained throughout the distribution network during periods of peak demand. 
7 
 
8 
Q. 
Please provide examples of Gas System Reinforcement projects. 
9 
A. 
Examples of System Reinforcement projects include: 
10 
• Replacing undersized mains with larger diameter mains (LPP is targeted 
11 
whenever practical during this work); 
12 
• Looping or connecting system endpoints by installing new main; 
13 
• System pressure uprates (e.g., 15 pounds per square inch (“psi”) to 60 
14 
psi); 
15 
• Installing new district regulators and replacing existing undersized 
16 
district regulators; and 
17 
• Transferring existing low-pressure customers to an adjacent high-
18 
pressure main. 
19

20

21

approximately $6.919 million in FY 2022, $7.066 million FY 2023, $7.215 
15 
million in FY 2024, and $7.367 million in FY 2024.

2 
 
3 
Q. 
Please describe the Transmission Services Removal Program. 
4 
A. 
This five-year program, which began in FY 2019, will remove 260 gas services 
5 
from Transmission Pipeline E-31 (“E-31 Services”), a 200 psig transmission 
6 
pipeline located in Saratoga County, and connect them to distribution main.  
7 
The program involves transferring 44 of the E-31 Services to nearby existing 
8 
distribution main and extending 10.1 miles of new distribution main to serve 
9 
the remaining 216 customers that are located more than 200 feet from existing 
10 
distribution main.  
11 
 
12 
Q. 
Why is this program necessary? 
13 
A. 
From 2013 to 2015, the Company reviewed the E-31 Services through its 
14 
Process Hazard Analysis (“PHA”) to determine the overall process safety risk 
15 
associated with the E-31 Services and concluded that the cumulative safety risk 
16 
exceeded the Company’s internal process safety risk threshold.  The PHA 
17 
utilized a Layers of Protection Analysis (“LOPA”) technique developed by the 
18 
Center for Chemical Process Safety (“CCPS”) based on equipment failure 
19 
scenarios, such as third-party excavator damage, pressure regulator failure, non-
20 
gas related structure fires, vehicular damage to above ground equipment, and 
21 


--- Page 65 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 63 of 105 
 
 
 
weather-related failures, to determine the quantitative risk level for each failure 
1 
scenario.  At the completion of the risk assessment process, the Company 
2 
concluded the total cumulative failure risk for the E-31 Services exceeds the 
3 
Company’s internal risk guidelines due to insufficient layers of protection and, 
4 
therefore, relocation of these services to distribution main is necessary.   
5 
 
6 
Q. 
What capital investments are required for this program? 
7 
A. 
The program involves installation of new distribution pipelines, new 
8 
distribution rated service lines, and the decommissioning, abandoning and 
9 
securing of each transmission service tee.  The cost estimate for this project is 
10 
$4.288 million in FY 2022 and $4.365 million in FY 2023. 
11 
 
12 
Q. 
Is the Company taking steps to mitigate the risk associated with the E-31 
13 
Services prior to the Rate Year? 
14 
A. 
Yes.  The Company replaced the existing service regulators with high pressure 
15 
service regulators in FY 2018.  Starting FY 2019, the Company began 
16 
relocating services to new and existing distribution mains while abandoning 
17 
Transmission Services.  As of FY 2020, the Company abandoned 48 
18 
Transmission Services and is on schedule to abandon an additional 90 services 
19 
in FY 2021.  Approximately 122 E-31 Services remain at this time and the 
20 


--- Page 66 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 64 of 105 
 
 
 
proposed program will retire or relocate those remaining services in the Rate 
1 
Year and Data Year 1.

16

The Proactive Main and Service Replacement (LPP) Program does not include

21

Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel

structures that require more than re-coating.  The complexity and level of 
1

Q. 
Please describe what is included in the Transmission Pipeline Reactive 
5 
Program. 
6 
A. 
This program covers the capital projects required to reactively mitigate integrity 
7 
related issues on gas transmission pipelines.  Integrity issues are identified by 
8 
the Company’s IMP, mandated inspections, and during normal operations.  
9 
These integrity issues can be caused by corrosion, third party damage, valve 
10 
failures, and other issues that affect the integrity of pipelines.  This program 
11 
covers mitigation projects that are more urgent or of a higher priority and that 
12 
are not addressed as part of the IMP workplan.  Projects consist of pipe 
13 
replacement by direct trenching and directional drill and may include valve 
14 
replacement.  A reactive program is required to address higher-risk 
15 
transmission integrity issues as they are discovered. 
16 
 
17 
Q. 
Please describe the Cross-Bore Investigation and Remediation Program. 
18 
A. 
A cross bore is an unintended consequence of horizontal directional drilling 
19 
(“HDD”).  It occurs when a plastic gas main compromises a sewer lateral that 
20 
was not identified during the gas installation process.  A cross bore can block 
21 


--- Page 68 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 66 of 105 
 
 
 
the sewer line, and any attempt to clear the blockage can damage the gas line 
1 
and cause gas to migrate into a building.  In recent years, several such incidents 
2 
have occurred in the industry and, as a result, many utilities have initiated 
3 
programs to identify and remedy this situation.   
4 
 
5 
The Company updated its HDD procedures in 2014 to address and eliminate 
6 
possible cross bores.  Historically, Niagara Mohawk’s drilling procedures and 
7 
the typical depth of sewer laterals in the Company’s service territory due to 
8 
frost permeation depths would have mitigated against the occurrence of cross 
9 
bores.  However, prior to 2014, the risk specific to cross bores was not known; 
10 
thus, the Company cannot determine with certainty that cross bores did not 
11 
occur absent inspection of pre-2014 installations.  This program includes the 
12 
inspection of known HDD jobs pre-2014 installations.   
13 
 
14 
Q. 
Please describe the Company’s proposal for the Large Diameter Main 
15 
Rehabilitation Program. 
16 
A. 
Similar to the program approved in the 2016 KEDNY & KEDLI Rate Case, 
17 
Niagara Mohawk proposes including a Large Diameter Main Rehabilitation 
18 
Program for cast iron and unprotected steel main in its capital plan.  This 
19 
program will utilize cured-in-place pipe lining (“CIP”) to cost effectively 
20 
improve the safety and reliability of its large diameter mains.  If it is impractical 
21 


--- Page 69 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 67 of 105 
 
 
 
to utilize this technology, Niagara Mohawk will replace that section of large 
1 
diameter pipe to maintain the integrity of the system.  This technology has 
2 
proven to extend the life of the main for more than 50 years, improve system 
3 
performance, and reduce future leaks on treated pipeline segments.  This large 
4 
diameter lining technology has been used for years in downstate New York.  
5 
The Company will include main segments reconditioned through CIP lining in 
6 
the inventory of LPP for eventual retirement.  The Large Diameter Main 
7 
Rehabilitation Program will address one mile per year using CIP lining at a cost 
8 
of approximately $6.488 million in FY 2024 and $6.618 million in FY 2025.  
9 
 
10 
Q. 
Please describe the Restrictions for Elevated Gas Infrastructure program. 
11 
A. 
This is a ten-year program to install fencing or other physical deterrents to 
12 
restrict and/or deter public access to elevated gas facilities (four feet or higher 
13 
above the ground or across a body of water).   The purpose of this program is 
14 
to reduce the risk to the public of climb and fall injuries or fatalities.  More 
15 
information on this program is provided in the testimony of the Gas Safety 
16 
Panel.

Q. 
Please describe the Transmission Pipeline Washouts Program. 
19 
A. 
During normal operations, gas transmission system pipelines can be exposed to 
20 
environmental conditions that can affect the integrity of the pipeline.  These 
21 


--- Page 72 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 70 of 105 
 
 
 
environmental conditions may include localized flooding, scouring/erosion of 
1 
stream bottoms under normal flow, and ground subsidence due to subsurface 
2 
geological activity.  Both federal and state regulations require operators to 
3 
perform continuing surveillance and follow-up mitigation activity to insure the 
4 
integrity of these pipelines.  Recent PHMSA Advisory Bulletins highlight 
5 
actions operators must take to ensure that flooding events, normal river scour 
6 
and river channel migration do not affect integrity of pipelines.  These Advisory 
7 
Bulletins also outline actions needed after severe storms, such as hurricanes. 
8 
 
9 
This program covers the capital projects required to mitigate the effects of 
10 
environmental damage to existing gas transmission pipelines.  Projects consist 
11 
of pipe replacement by direct trenching, directional drilling, and civil 
12 
engineering repairs.   
13 
 
14 
Q. 
Please describe what is included in the Valve Installation and Replacement 
15 
Program. 
16 
A. 
Federal and state regulations require installation, inspection, operation and 
17 
maintenance of critical pipeline valves on all gas distribution systems.  The 
18 
purpose of these valves is to facilitate the rapid shutdown of distribution piping 
19 
during gas emergencies such as third-party damage or water intrusion.  A 
20 


--- Page 73 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 71 of 105 
 
 
 
secondary purpose of these valves is to facilitate maintenance and pipe 
1 
replacement on associated distribution piping.   
2 
 
3 
This is an ongoing program to strengthen the Company’s emergency response 
4 
capabilities by replacing those valves found to be inoperable and unrepairable, 
5 
and thus improving the level at which Field Operations personnel can safely 
6 
and efficiently isolate sections of the distribution system while mitigating 
7 
customer impacts (e.g., reducing the extent and duration of future outages).  
8 
Ensuring all critical valves are properly maintained and operable is a key public 
9 
safety function and is essential to the effective operation of the Company’s gas 
10 
distribution system.  The FY 2022 forecast of approximately $0.111 million 
11 
each year, adjusted for inflation in each of FY 2203 –FY 2025. 
12 
 
13 
Q. 
Please describe what is included in the Purchase Meters Program. 
14 
A. 
This program includes the purchase, testing, processing and delivery of gas 
15 
meters and associated instrumentation needed to support the Meter Change 
16 
Program and new customer connections programs.

4 
Q. 
Please describe the status of the previously proposed Albany Loop Closure 
5 
project. 
6 
A. 
The Albany Loop Closure project is a potential option to enhance reliability for 
7 
approximately 50,000 customers in the event of a loss of gas supply and to 
8 
address vulnerability concerns in the East Gate region.  The project will provide 
9 
increased reliability to the Company’s gas system by allowing more gas to flow 
10 
through the Tennessee Gas Pipeline’s (“TGP”) South Albany city gate station 
11 
into the Albany transmission loop and will help to mitigate against the loss of 
12 
either the Dominion Transmission Inc. (“DTI”) Troy city gate or the upstream 
13 
supply to the Troy city gate.  The project will address “East Gate” vulnerability 
14 
concerns by allowing additional gas supplies into the gas system and reducing 
15 
the system’s dependence on DTI’s currently constrained gas system in the 
16 
northeastern part of the Company’s gas system.  This project involves 
17 
approximately 7.3 miles of 16-inch natural gas transmission main in the Towns 
18 
of North Greenbush and East Greenbush in Rensselaer County, and the Town 
19 
of Bethlehem in Albany County.   
20 
 
21 


--- Page 77 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 75 of 105 
 
 
 
In the near term, the Company has determined that there are adequate gas 
1 
supplies to meet customer demand in upstate New York, such that we are able 
2 
to temporarily defer the Albany Loop project from a supply perspective – but 
3 
the project would still provide significant reliability benefits.  The Company 
4 
proposes to spend $0.750 million in FY 2022 to continue project development 
5 
and in-progress engineering, and, if the project is determined to be required for 
6 
future reliability, $4.000 million in FY 2023, $40.000 million in FY 2024, and 
7 
$35.000 million in FY 2025 for material procurement and construction.

Q. 
Please describe what is included in the I&R Reactive Program. 
10 
A. 
The reactive I&R budget provides funding for capital investment in pressure 
11 
regulating and control stations.  Typical projects in this category include 
12

13

--- Page 78 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 76 of 105 
 
 
 
Q. 
Please describe what is included in the Heater Installation and Pressure 
1 
Regulating Facilities Programs. 
2 
A. 
There are 41 natural gas heaters currently operating on the Company’s system.  
3 
Because high-pressure gas cools when reduced to a lower pressure, heaters are 
4 
required at pressure regulating stations to prevent freeze-ups that can impact 
5 
flow control devices.  In addition, cold gas temperatures can lead to reduced 
6 
pipe toughness and increased potential for frost heave and cold temperature-
7 
induced stresses.  The heater program adds new heaters (where required) and 
8 
replaces existing heaters that have reached the end of their useful lives.   
9 
 
10 
The Pressure Regulating Facilities Program provides funding for replacement 
11 
and/or rebuilding and reconditioning of existing regulating and control stations.  
12 
Pressure regulating facilities (or stations) are designed to control system 
13 
pressures and maintain continuity of supply during normal operating conditions 
14 
and during periods of peak gas demand.   
15 
 
16 
Niagara Mohawk has assessed regulating stations on its system, evaluating 
17 
factors such as pressure, location, including whether the station is located in a 
18 
FEMA 100 year or 500-year flood zone, and the number of dependent 
19 
customers for each station.  In addition, the assessment considered station 
20 
condition, including pipe corrosion, location and type of overpressure 
21 


--- Page 79 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 77 of 105 
 
 
 
protection, automation, condition of vaults, vault covers, wall sleeves, piping 
1 
vents and ladders.  The results of the assessment were used to create an overall 
2 
risk rating for each station that serves as the basis for prioritizing projects in 
3 
this program.  The program includes full or partial replacement of existing 
4 
stations, and storm hardening upgrades where necessary. 
5 
 
6 
The investments in the Pressure Regulating Facilities Program do not cover the 
7 
special project capital improvements to specific stations that are separately set 
8 
forth in the investment plan and described below. 
9 
 
10 
Q. 
Please describe the Pressure Regulation Special Projects Program. 
11 
A. 
Pressure Regulation Special Projects are capital investments to address 
12 
reliability issues at specific stations that are separately budgeted and are not 
13 
included in any other blanket reliability programs.  These projects are described 
14 
in detail in the “Pressure Regulation Special Projects” data sheet included in 
15 
Exhibit __ (GIOP-4).  The projects target facilities that have the highest 
16

17 
odorant system upgrades, installation of gas quality verification equipment, 
18 
installation of remote shut-off capabilities, and overpressure protection.

--- Page 80 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 78 of 105 
 
 
 
Q. 
Please describe what is included in the System Automation category. 
1 
A. 
This program will install updated Remote Terminal Units (“RTUs”) at multiple 
2 
city gate and regulator stations.  RTUs provide temperature, pressure and flow 
3 
data back to the Gas Control Room.  RTUs can also monitor gas detectors and 
4 
intrusion alarms and allow Gas Control Operators to adjust flow and pressure 
5 
set points at regulator stations.  The benefits include enhanced calibration of 
6 
network models from automation and telemetry data, improved accuracy of 
7 
network analysis, and enhanced ability to forecast the need for capital 
8 
reinforcements, which will lead to more efficient capital planning.  Automation 
9 
allows Gas Control Operators to selectively close valves, raise or lower 
10 
pressures, and shut down gate stations.  System alarms also alert Gas Control 
11 
Operators to system issues and allow quick pinpointing of the source.  
12 
 
13 
PHMSA regulations regarding Control Room Management require Operators 
14 
to ensure that “practices and procedures within their control rooms are adequate 
15 
to maintain pipeline safety and integrity.”  These rules indicate that Operators 
16 
should have telemetry to monitor pipelines, as it would increase system 
17 
awareness and enable a proactive response to abnormal operating conditions.  
18 
The System Automation program complies with these regulations by providing 
19 
for increased deployment of telemetry on the Company’s system. 
20 
 
21 


--- Page 81 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 79 of 105 
 
 
 
Currently, Niagara Mohawk’s pressure regulation stations are equipped with 
1 
some form of telemetry, but not all are equipped with remote control 
2 
functionality, and some of telemetry equipment installed is dated and obsolete.  
3 
Niagara Mohawk’s System Automation program adds automation capabilities 
4 
to low pressure regulator stations and updates RTU equipment and 
5 
communications capabilities.  This program will provide enhanced ability to 
6 
monitor system performance and remotely adjust pressures on the gas system.   
7 
 
8 
Q. 
Please describe what is included in the Gas System Reliability – Gas 
9 
Planning/Remote Controlled Valve (“RCV”) Program. 
10 
A. 
This program includes capital projects required to maintain system minimum 
11 
pressures on the gas network in the event of an abnormal operating condition 
12 
(failure involving a regulator station, gate station, critical main, or other major 
13 
pressure facility on the system).  Gas System Planning ensures that customers 
14 
continue to have reliable service and that no customers experience interruptions 
15 
as a result of an unplanned outage of a facility under normal winter conditions.  
16 
The program also involves the installation of additional RCVs on transmission 
17 
pipelines to improve emergency response capability and reduce the risk of gas 
18 
releases.  In the event of a pipeline failure, RCVs allow control room operators 
19 
to stop the flow of gas and to remotely isolate and shut down a portion of the 
20 
system.  Improving response time through the expanded deployment of RCVs 
21 


--- Page 82 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 80 of 105 
 
 
 
reduces the quantity of gas released and can limit the harm to the public and 
1 
property.   
2 
 
3 
Q. 
Please provide examples of Gas Planning Reliability projects. 
4 
A. 
Examples of Gas Planning Reliability projects include: integrating distribution 
5 
systems with the same operating pressures through pipeline connections, 
6 
expanding supply diversity, and projects targeting areas of the system where 
7 
large numbers of customers would experience a service interruption if a single 
8 
gas facility became inoperable when the average daily temperature is five 
9 
degrees Fahrenheit. 
10 
 
11 
Q. 
Please describe what is included in the Water Intrusion and Distribution 
12 
Main Exposure Program. 
13 
A. 
The Water Intrusion and Distribution Main Exposure Program is designed to 
14 
address water entering the gas distribution system, resulting in main 
15 
obstructions, poor pressure and/or freezing customer services, and 
16 
undermines/exposures of distribution mains due to flooding, heavy rains, and 
17 
running streams.  This reactive program also targets the retirement of LPP that 
18 
is susceptible to water intrusion but is not prioritized for replacement under 
19 
other main replacement programs because of the absence of leaks and/or 
20 
historic leak repair activity. 
21 


--- Page 83 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 81 of 105 
 
 
 
 
1 
Q. 
Please describe the proposed capital investment in Odorant Vapor 
2 
Management at Gate Stations. 
3 
A. 
All of the Company’s gate stations have large odorant bulk tanks to inject 
4 
odorant, mercaptan, into gas before it enters the Company’s system.  The 
5 
odorant is incredibly pungent and helps to alert the Company and the public of 
6 
gas leaks.  The odorant tanks at the Company’s gate stations are presently 
7 
secured and have spill mitigation measures available; however, the Company 
8 
does not have in place a mechanism to control fumes that may escape during 
9 
periodic maintenance or in the rare event of a spill.  This program proposes to 
10 
install equipment at each of the Company’s 24 gate stations that will prevent 
11 
the fumes from spreading into nearby communities.  The primary benefit of this 
12 
program will be a reduction in public false odor complaints, which can overload 
13 
the Company’s call center resources and require unnecessary field visits.  The 
14 
estimated cost of this program is $25,000 per gate station.  The Company 
15 
proposes to spread the costs of the program over four years, FY 2022 – FY 
16 
2025.  Specifically, the Company proposes to spend $0.050 million in FY 2022, 
17 
$0.200 million in each of FY 2023 and FY 2024 , and $0.150 million in FY 
18 
2025. 
19 
 
20

Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel

Q. 
Please describe the Gas Regulator Station Security Project. 
1 
A. 
This project will continue to enhance and improve security measures at critical 
2 
gas pressure reducing stations that are not scheduled for full station replacement.  
3 
In December 2016, PHMSA issued an Advisory Bulletin (AB-2016-06) 
4 
recommending enhanced security for critical energy infrastructure following an 
5 
incident on an interstate gas pipeline.  The project targets 55 regulator stations 
6 
that are critical to reliable operation of the system.  Niagara Mohawk will assess 
7 
these key city gate and regulator stations to determine vulnerabilities to vandals, 
8 
activists, out of control vehicles, and other external physical threats to safe 
9 
operations.  The results of the security assessments, combined with the relative 
10 
importance of the facility to reliable and safe operation of the overall 
11 
transmission and distribution system, will determine the level and types of 
12 
security enhancements.  Examples of security measures include remotely 
13 
operated cameras connected to gas control centers, lighting, fencing, ID card 
14 
access, intrusion alarms, redundant communications systems, physical barriers, 
15 
and hardened locks and cables to protect exposed valves and equipment.   
16 
 
17

Q. 
Please describe Instrumentation and Regulation (“I&R”) and the need for 
4 
the Company’s I&R safety proposals. 
5 
A. 
I&R involves the safe and reliable operation of critical gas infrastructure used 
6 
to maintain appropriate gas system pressures, including pressure regulating 
7 
stations, heaters, gas quality and odorization equipment, pressure control and 
8 
pressure monitoring, and system automation equipment.  The recent incident in 
9 
Merrimack Valley, Massachusetts involved an over pressure event on the 
10 
utility’s gas distribution system.  Following that incident, at the direction of 
11 
Staff, New York gas utilities immediately reviewed their pressure regulation 
12 
and overpressure protection assets and procedures to identify system risks, 
13 
identify mitigation actions, and consider best practices, including those 
14 
recommended by Staff.  The Company’s review identified areas where best 
15 
practices were already in place, and recommended improvements and 
16 
enhancements to improve the safety and reliability of I&R assets.  As a result, 
17 
in the testimony of this Panel and the Gas Safety Panel, the Company is 
18 
proposing several I&R safety programs designed to improve procedures, 
19 
information availability and standardization, knowledge transfer, employee 
20 
competencies, public safety, and over pressure risk reduction.   
21 


--- Page 87 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 85 of 105 
 
 
 
 
1 
Q. 
Please describe Niagara Mohawk’s Distribution Station Over Pressure 
2 
Protection Program. 
3 
A. 
As exhibited by the Merrimack Valley incident, preventing over pressurization 
4 
is vital for the safe operation of the Company’s gas system.  This program will 
5 
install additional and replace obsolete protective devices at regulator stations 
6 
on the Company’s system to reduce the risk of over pressurization.  Specifically, 
7 
this program will install override pilots at regulator stations where external 
8 
control lines are susceptible to third-party damage or isolation that could result 
9 
in an over pressure event.  In addition, as part of this program, the Company 
10 
will replace antiquated oil seal relief devices with modern mechanical relief 
11 
devices to deliver increased reliability and functionality.  In the event of an over 
12 
pressurization, the operation of a mechanical relief device provides reductions 
13 
in carbon emissions as compared to the oil seal and will eliminate the possibility 
14 
of oil contamination.  The Company will begin installation of override pilots in 
15 
FY 2021, and the program will continue through FY 2022 – FY 2025 to address 
16 
all stations.  The capital investment required for this program is approximately 
17 
$0.763 million in FY 2022, $0.778 million in FY 2023, $0.529 million in FY 
18 
2024, and $0.540 million in FY 2025. 
19 
 
20
