Q. 
Please generally describe the nature of the Company’s gas system O&M 
8 
expenses. 
9 
A. 
O&M expenses relate to work performed to provide customer support, respond 
10 
to emergencies, perform safety inspections and other compliance activities, 
11 
restore service, and maintain the life of capital assets.  The Company has a 
12 
significant maintenance program to ensure that system assets are utilized to 
13 
their fullest potential life expectancy.  As gas facilities age, maintenance costs 
14 
increase.  These costs include more frequent inspection and testing required by 
15 
regulatory changes, increased volume of repairs, more significant repair work, 
16 
increased emergency work, and gas pipeline safety enhancements.  These 
17 
expenditures are required to prevent failure and maintain the life of the assets 
18 
until replacement occurs.  The Company’s O&M programs are also designed to 
19 
maintain the service commitments in its gas safety performance metrics, which 
20 
cover various aspects of its performance in the areas of reliability and safety, 
21 


--- Page 92 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel

including metrics measuring pipeline replacement, emergency response, leak 
1 
management, and damage prevention.

i. 
Capital Support - General 
4 
Q. 
What O&M services will the various construction support functions 
5 
provide to support the Company’s increased capital investments? 
6 
A. 
Construction support functions include internal groups providing contract 
7 
administration, project management, budgeting, and resource planning.  While 
8 
the majority of costs from these functions are directly charged to capital 
9 
projects, the Company incurs O&M expenses for items such as training, travel, 
10 
conferences, licensing, new employee on-boarding, and costs for administering 
11 
O&M contracts.  The Company estimates that approximately ten percent of 
12 
construction support employees’ time is O&M expense.   
13 
 
 
14 


--- Page 97 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 95 of 105 
 
 
 
As Niagara Mohawk increases its capital expenditures, the Company will 
1 
require additional capital support resources, including gas system engineering 
2 
and asset management (engineers), and capital delivery and operations support 
3 
(program managers, planners, supervisors, analysts) ). 
4 
 
5 
Q. 
What are the incremental support requirements in the Rate Year? 
6 
A. 
The Company forecasts approximately 14 FTEs for these support functions in 
7 
the Rate Year.  A breakdown of the positions is provided in Exhibit __ (GIOP-
8 
6).

ii. 
I&R 
11 
Q. 
Please describe the I&R Supervisor Onboarding Initiative. 
12 
A. 
The I&R Supervisor Onboarding initiative requests two FTEs that will work in 
13 
the field with existing I&R supervisors, mechanics, and technicians to gain on-
14 
site training and learn the complexities of gas regulator stations and the 
15 
associated distribution and transmission pipeline system.  These employees will 
16 
also be trained to oversee the safe operation and management of compressed 
17 
natural gas (“CNG”) injection plants on the Company’s system.  The primary 
18 
purpose of this initiative is succession planning, as these supervisor positions 
19 
typically take several years to gain the appropriate expertise and the loss of a 
20 
current experienced supervisor could create a significant reduction in 
21 


--- Page 98 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 96 of 105 
 
 
 
supervisory staff.  These incremental employees have been included in Exhibit 
1 
__ (GIOP-6). 
2 
 
3 
iii. 
IMP/IVP 
4 
Q. 
Please describe the O&M requirements of the IMP and IVP Programs. 
5 
A. 
Incremental non-labor O&M in the amount of $3.485 million in the Rate Year, 
6 
$1.863 million in Data Year 1, and $2.008 million in Data Year 2, as shown in 
7 
Exhibit___(GIOP-5), and an additional 5.5 FTEs in the Rate Year and Data 
8 
Years are required to conduct the required inspections via ECDA on pipelines 
9 
that are not ILI-enabled and to conduct ILI on those that are already enabled.  
10 
These costs include excavation and support for ECDA inspections, evaluation 
11 
of testing data and the costs of non-capital repairs such as repair sleeves and on-
12 
site material testing.  For IVP, these costs are needed for material verification, 
13 
long seam evaluation program, and other costs associated with new PHMSA 
14 
rule making.  The costs of the FTEs are shown in Exhibit___(RRP-3), Schedule 
15 
27 and supported by the Revenue Requirements Panel (“RRP”).

7

8

incremental FTEs in the Rate Year and Data Years to conduct records reviews 
10

10

11 
to determine the extent of and address corrosion in the Rate Year and Data 
12

13

14 
expanded corrosion control activities.  If approved, the Company intends to 
15 
immediately hire these FTEs to ensure they are available by the beginning of 
16 
the Rate Year.  The costs for FTEs are shown in Exhibit___(RRP-3), Schedule 
17 
27 and supported by the Revenue Requirements Panel.

2 
VI. 
Gas Business Enablement 
3 
Q. 
What is the GBE Program? 
4 
A. 
The GBE Program is a program to develop and implement a comprehensive 
5 
framework of new technology solutions and business process changes 
6 
necessary to strengthen and improve the performance of National Grid’s U.S. 
7 
gas business.  Specifically, the GBE Program will replace a number of aged 
8 
computer systems, support continuous improvement in gas safety performance 
9 


--- Page 103 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 101 of 105 
 
 
 
including the implementation of American Petroleum Institute Recommended 
1 
Practice 1173, facilitate more effective and efficient delivery of the overall 
2 
work plan, and progress the satisfaction of evolving customer expectations. 
3 
 
4 
Q. 
Did the Joint Proposal adopted by the Commission in the 2017 Rate Case 
5 
address the GBE Program?   
6 
A. 
Yes.  The Joint Proposal authorized Niagara Mohawk to implement the GBE 
7 
Program and recover program costs subject to certain customer protections.  
8 
The protections included (i) a Program Total Cost Cap that capped Niagara 
9 
Mohawk’s allocable share of GBE Program costs at $458.1 million through FY 
10 
2023 (comprised of $293.6 million of capital expense and $164.5 million of 
11 
O&M expense); (ii) a downward-only Service Company Rents Net Utility Plant 
12 
and Depreciation Expense Reconciliation Mechanism applicable to GBE 
13 
Program capital investments; and (iii) six key performance indicators (“KPIs”) 
14 
that would be measured at the end of FY 2023 and result in the return of a 
15 
portion of certain investments to customers if the KPIs were not met.   
16 
 
17 
Q. 
What is the current status of the GBE Program at Niagara Mohawk?  
18 
A. 
National Grid had originally planned to implement certain GBE functionality, 
19 
including Portfolio Anchor 3.1, at Niagara Mohawk in April 2020.  However, 
20 
as described in the Company’s quarterly GBE status report dated June 1, 2020 
21 


--- Page 104 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 102 of 105 
 
 
 
in Case 17-G-0239, given the impacts of the COVID-19 pandemic on the 
1 
Company and its customers, and the need to focus on essential work necessary 
2 
to maintain safe and reliable service and safeguarding the health of its 
3 
workforce, customers, and communities, National Grid was forced to pause the 
4 
planned “go-live” date for this release.  Based on currently available 
5 
information on the anticipated impacts of the COVID-19 pandemic, the planned 
6 
release for Niagara Mohawk has been rescheduled to the fall of 2020.  The 
7 
Company continues to monitor the development of the pandemic and will adjust 
8 
the plans, as necessary, as the situation evolves.      
9 
 
10 
Q. 
Does this delay in the go-live date for Niagara Mohawk have an impact on 
11 
subsequent releases as well as total program costs? 
12 
A. 
Yes.  The decision to defer the release due to the COVID-19 pandemic has a 
13 
cascading impact on other planned releases due to timeline compression and 
14 
resulted in increased program costs.  To ensure success in delivering the 
15 
remaining releases, the Company is currently undergoing a comprehensive 
16 
review of the impact on the subsequent releases and alternative approaches that 
17 
will enable the GBE Program to move forward with contingencies.  Exhibit __ 
18 
(GIOP-10) lays out the current GBE Program Roadmap.  In addition, as of June 
19 
1, 2020, the Company’s best estimate of the updated budget forecast for the 
20 
GBE Program was $569 million; however, given the substantial impacts the 
21 


--- Page 105 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 103 of 105 
 
 
 
pandemic and other factors have had on costs and schedules, the Company is 
1 
looking to re-sanction the program this fall. 
2 
 
3 
Q. 
Will Niagara Mohawk customers be impacted by the increase in costs? 
4 
A. 
No.  Although program costs have increased due to circumstances beyond the 
5 
Company’s control, the Company is not requesting funding for the additional 
6 
program costs. 
7 
 
8 
Q. 
Is the Company proposing any changes to the customer protections 
9 
adopted by the Commission in the 2017 Rate Case? 
10 
A. 
Yes.  The Company is proposing two changes.  First, as discussed by the 
11 
Revenue Requirements Panel, while GBE Program costs have increased, the 
12 
proportional allocation of those costs to Niagara Mohawk has decreased.  
13 
Applying the lower allocation percentage to the Program Total Cost Cap would 
14 
result in a lower allocation of costs to Niagara Mohawk than was established in 
15 
the 2017 Rate Case.  Therefore, the Company is proposing to update the 
16 
Program Total Cost Cap such that Niagara Mohawk’s updated allocation results 
17 
in the allocation of costs at the same level approved by the Commission in the 
18 
2017 Rate Case.  In this way, customers will pay no more and no less than the 
19 
costs approved by the Commission for recovery.   
20 
 
21 


--- Page 106 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 104 of 105 
 
 
 
 
Second, because work has been paused due to COVID-19, the Company is 
1 
proposing to extend measurement of the KPIs to year end FY 2024 to align with 
2 
the new expected in-service date of the GBE Program in Niagara Mohawk’s 
3 
service territory.  This change is supported by the terms of the Joint Proposal, 
4 
which provides at Section IV.7.8.3: 
5 
In the event of any unforeseen circumstances, changes in law or 
6 
regulation, or other events that impact the underlying 
7 
assumptions used to develop the GBE KPI performance targets, 
8 
such that the KPI targets are no longer reasonable measures of 
9 
the Company’s performance, the Company and Staff may, by 
10 
mutual agreement subject to Commission approval, equitably 
11 
adjust the GBE KPI targets upward or downward to address such 
12 
changes.  In addition, the Company’s failure to perform under 
13 
any GBE KPI will be excused if the Company can reasonably 
14 
demonstrate that the failure to perform was caused by any 
15 
unanticipated or anomalous circumstances or events outside of 
16 
the Company’s control and materially affected or delayed the 
17 
Company’s performance with respect to the affected KPI. 
18 
 
19 
The COVID-19 pandemic and its effects were unforeseen at the time of the 
20 
Joint Proposal and the 2017 Rate Case and have affected the Company’s ability 
21 
to implement the GBE Program such that this adjustment to the GBE 
22 
performance targets is warranted. 
23 
 
24 
Q. 
What are the costs related to the GBE Program that are included in the 
25 
revenue requirements?   
26 
A. 
As shown in Exhibit__(RRP-11), Workpapers to Exhibit__(RRP-3), Schedule 
27 
9, Workpapers 3, 6 and 9, the Service Company capital investment for the GBE 
28 


--- Page 107 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 105 of 105 
 
 
 
Program upon which a related rent expense will be allocated to Niagara 
1 
Mohawk is $390.8 million along with Niagara Mohawk’s allocable share of 
2 
$140.2 million of related O&M expense.  The Company proposes to continue 
3 
the downward-only Service Company Rents Net Utility Plant and Depreciation 
4 
Expense Reconciliation Mechanism applicable to GBE Program capital 
5 
investments during the rate plan.   
6 
 
7 
Q. 
Does this conclude your testimony? 
8 
A. 
Yes, it does.

--- Page 118 ---
 
 
Program Title:  Customer Connections  
 
Spending Rationale: 
 
  Mandated 
  Customer Connections 
  Reliability 
 Non-Infrastructure
 
Brief Description: 
 
The Customer Connections program includes new construction was well as converting 
customers from oil, propane or electric resistance heating to natural gas.  The program 
involves the installation of new main, services and meters that the Company is required to 
install pursuant to its tariff to serve projected customer gas requests.   
 
Program Justification: 
 
This program provides significant customer and societal benefits.  The Company plans to 
invest $126.1 million on the program over the next five years, from fiscal year 2021 
through 2025, or about $25 million per year.  This investment will create approximately 
127 direct external jobs in construction and related industries as workers plan and install 
gas main, lateral, meters and other equipment necessary to connect approximately 2,400 
new residential and commercial gas customers each year.  In addition, an estimated 22 
indirect external jobs will be created in the supply chain (industries providing goods and 
services to the program) each year; and 32 induced external jobs will be created in local 
service industries as workers spend their income.  In total, the program is projected to 
support 181 external jobs per year.  Spending on the program is also projected to add $16.2 
million per year to the gross domestic product (GDP) in the service area, $11.3 million in 
additional personal income and $2.3 million in state & local tax revenue.

--- Page 119 ---
 
 
Besides the jobs, income, and GDP created while installing the new gas services consisting 
of approximately 2,350 installations for new construction and oil conversions and 50 
installations for propane conversions to natural gas.  Residential gas customers will save 
over $1,000 per year in annual energy costs compared to oil and over $2,000 per year 
compared to using propane. The typical business customer, using 461 Dth per year, will 
save over $6,000 in annual energy costs compared to oil.  This amounts to $5.7 million per 
year in energy cost savings for all 2,400 customers connected each year.  These new 
connections will also reduce CO2 emissions by 14,841 tons per year while displacing 2.9 
million gallons of oil per year.  
 
Energy cost savings put more money in business and consumer pockets, boosting regional 
competitiveness and local spending on goods and services.  This leads to more local 
economic activity and increased jobs, income and GDP.  In total, energy cost savings to 
residents and businesses are projected to create 46 permanent external jobs per year in the 
service territory while increasing local GDP by $4.2 million per year, real personal income 
by $3.6 million per year and state and local tax revenues by $0.7 million per year.  The 
breakdown of these savings between the 2,350 installations for new construction/oil 
conversions and the 50 propane conversions is shown below: 
 
Customer Benefits:  2,350 Service Connections:  New Construction and Oil Conversions 
 
2,350 new gas service connections (2,175 residential and 175 business) have positive 
economic benefits and environmental impacts: 
• $5.5 million annual customer energy savings  
• 23 tons of local emissions reduced (NOx, SO2, VOC, NH3 and PM2.5) 
• 14,841 tons of CO2 reduced 
• 2.9 million gallons of oil displaced (68,774 barrels) 
• Same as taking 35,000 cars off the road for one year 
• $4.2 million annual GDP created 
• $3.5 million annual income created 
• 45 annual jobs created 
• $0.7 million state and local tax revenues 
 
Per Customer 
• $2,347 annual energy savings 
• 1,229 gallons of oil eliminated 
• $1,767 individual GDP created 
• Equivalent emissions reduction to removing 15 cars off the road 
 
Customer Benefits:  50 Service Connections-- Propane Conversions 
 
50 new residential propane conversions to natural gas have positive economic benefits: 
• $162,000 annual customer energy savings  
• $96,000 annual GDP created 
• $61,000 annual income created 
Exhibit__(GIOP-4) 
Page 2 of 136


--- Page 120 ---
 
 
• 1 annual job created 
• $12,000 state and local tax revenues 
 
Per Customer 
• $3,235 annual energy savings 
• $1,920 individual GDP created 
 
Conversions.  The Company’s forecast shows steady demand in the residential conversion 
market due to low natural gas prices compared to oil and propane.  The commercial 
conversion market is forecasted to decrease slightly due to market saturation and fewer 
large project opportunities.  Roughly 66% of the work resides in the Company’s Eastern 
Division leaving the remaining 34% in Central Division.  All customer requests for natural 
gas are evaluated by estimated load and reviewed by engineering to validate there are no 
capacity issues.  Changes in the sales mix to mostly residential conversion projects will 
require more capital spending per new customer.   Overall, the gas price advantage will 
increase from 2021-2025 and this will provide continued demand for gas.  
 
 
Source:  Actual NMPC filed residential gas heating rates through November 2020.  Actual NMPC area heating oil and 
propane prices from NSERDA (average of the Capital, North and Central regions) through March 2020.  Prices 
escalated, 2021-2035, using the US Energy Administration (EIA) 2020 energy price outlook for the Mid Atlantic region. 
 
New Construction.  Moody’s is forecasting an increase in new construction activity in the 
Company’s service territory which will increase demand for new gas service from 2021-
2025.  After falling slightly from 2010 to 2019, housing starts are expected to rise sharply 
in 2020 through 2023 before leveling off.  Roughly 68% of the work resides in the 
Company’s Eastern Division leaving the remaining 32% in Central Division.  All requests 
for natural gas are evaluated by estimated load and reviewed by engineering to validate 
there are no capacity issues.  Customer Connections work in the service territory is 
expected to rise real GDP to 1.4% per from 2019-2027 compared to an average annual rise 
of 0.9% from 2010-2019.   The employment in the service territory is expected to grow 
0.2% per year, which is slightly less than the 2010-2019 historical rate of 0.6%.  Real 
income per capita is expected to rise at 2.9% per year, about equal to the historical rate.

--- Page 121 ---
 
 
 
 
 
 
The forecast considers the implications of (a) changes in the various market segments; (b) 
large project inventories; (c) rate/regulatory changes; and (d) system constraints.   The 
forecast also considers factors that drive projections and the associated capital investments: 
• Fuel Pricing – oil versus natural gas 
• Inventory levels and turnover ratios  
• Saturation levels  
• Marketing Lead performance 
• Designs and resourcing that supports the delivery of capital at efficient 
pricing. 
• Economic Conditions / Building Starts 
• Gas system constraints

Program Title: Meter Purchases 
 
Spending Rationale: 
 
  Mandated 
  Customer Connections

Brief Description: 
These programs include the purchase, testing, processing, and delivery of traditional and smart gas 
meters, and associated instrumentation, to support the mandated Meter Test/Replacement 
Program, customer connections, and continued Customer Meter Service (CMS) operations. The 
estimated number of meters required to support these programs for FY21 - FY25 are:

Each year, National Grid is required to select randomly and remove from service a quantity of 
meters to be tested for accuracy. The number of meters removed and tested is sufficient to assure 
a statistical confidence level of 95%. Test results are entered into a program which performs the 
statistical calculations based upon an approved ANSI Standard. The NYS PSC has set accuracy 
limits for both Residential (AQL 10%), and Commercial (AQL 20%) meter types. Meter groups 
which fall beyond the specified limits are placed in a retirement program and are subsequently 
removed from service and retired.  
The purchase of smart gas meters is also included in the program beginning in FY22. The smart 
gas meters purchased as part of this program are independent of the Low Pressure Zone Smart 
Meter and Smart Meter AMI Integration Programs. A Smart Gas Meter contains safety 
functionality in addition to measuring gas usage including: over-pressure, over-temperature 
sensing, excess flow detection, advanced air/theft detection and integrated shutoff valve. The 
integrated shutoff valve can be triggered if any of the functionalities sense abnormal behavior as a 
standalone function controlled by the programmable meter logic. The communication device is 
housed within the meter, eliminating the need to purchase separate devices. The meter purchase 
programs will purchase smart meters, which will be deployed strategically throughout the region 
alongside traditional meters.

This program includes only the cost of the meters and does not include the installation cost of 
meters (see Meter Change program). 
 
Program Justification: 
The primary driver for meter and metering instrumentation purchases is compliance with state 
regulations governing meter accuracy and measurement of gas usage for customer bills.  
New York State PSC requirements stipulate a random sample and associated remediation/ 
retirement program for installed gas meters. 
In addition to the mandated meter change program, meters are required to support customer 
connections, as well as to support CMS operational requirements (load change, meter and/or 
service relocations, damage, & stopped meters).

Program Title:  Gas System Reinforcement Program 
 
Spending Rationale: 
 
  Mandated 
  Growth 
  Reliability 
Non-Infrastructure
 
Brief Description: 
 
The System Reinforcement Program consists of capital reinforcement projects required to 
maintain pressure above system minimums on the gas network during periods of peak 
demand, thereby maintaining continuous service to all gas customers within the territory.  
This program is a five-year program covering the winter periods for 2020/21 through 
2024/25. 
 
Program Justification:   
 
Federal Code 49 CFR 192.623 and New York State Code 16 NYCRR 255.623 require the 
Company to maintain minimum pressures on the gas system necessary to maintain 
reliable service to all firm customers.  The System Reinforcement Program identifies 
projects required to maintain service under peak day, peak hour conditions.  Niagara 
Mohawk’s gas system is designed for a peak day with an average temperature of -10°F 
(75HDD – Heating Degree Days) with five percent of the daily send-out as a peak hour.  
The peak demand is based on the same forecast utilized to develop the gas supply 
portfolio, and the Gas System Reinforcement program is a critical component for 
enabling delivery of that gas supply to firm customers.  In some cases, even small to 
moderate increases in the overall forecast can result in significant reinforcements due to 
certain regions experiencing high growth rates while other regions may be experiencing 
low growth rates or decreasing system demands.  
 
The Advanced Data Analytics (“ADA”) group’s load forecast shows an increasing trend 
as demonstrated in the below graph.

Examples of distribution system reinforcement projects include, but are not limited to, 
the following: 
• Replacing existing undersized mains with larger diameter mains.  Leak-prone pipe 
is targeted whenever practical 
• Looping or connecting system endpoints by installing new main 
• Installing new district regulators as well as replacing and/or rebuilding existing 
undersized district regulators 
• Transferring existing customers supplied from low-pressure mains to adjacent high-
pressure mains (i.e., load shedding).

Customer Benefit: 
 
By installing these reinforcements, the Company is ensuring that service is maintained to 
all firm gas customers on the system.  Without the reinforcement program, as many as 
22,205 customers are at risk of experiencing pressures below minimum design pressures 
and, therefore, at risk of losing service.  The estimated cost to relight these customers is 
$22.2M (approximately $1,000 per customer based on previous experiences).  A 
secondary benefit of the program is the elimination of leak-prone pipe wherever 
practicable.  For example, the program represents a replacement rate of about 7.9%, 
approximately 6,576 feet (1.25 miles), of leak-prone pipe in the five-year plan.  
 
Alternatives 
 
Alternative 1:  Do Nothing 
 
This alternative is rejected because 22,205 customers are predicted to experience 
pressures below minimum design levels and be at risk of losing service if design 
conditions were to be experienced during the five-year heating season term under the 
current Gas Supply send-out forecast.

department.  Additionally, ADA provided a forecast at a zip code level.  There is a high 
degree of confidence in the accuracy of the modeling and forecast and that the 
appropriate reinforcement projects were identified.

  Mandated 
  Customer Connections

Brief Description: 
Following the September 13, 2018 incident involving Columbia Gas, the National Transportation 
Safety Board issued safety recommendations which recommended that a licensed PE review and 
approve engineering plans for all gas system work.  Currently, other states within the National 
Grid service territory (MA and RI) have passed legislation requiring PE stamping of gas design 
work.  If similar legislation is passed in NY, the assumption is that the guidance for the type of 
work requiring a PE stamp will remain consistent with the guidance being used in MA and RI.

Program Title:  Public Works Program (City/State Construction) 
 
Spending Rationale: 
 
  Mandated 
  Customer Connections 
  Reliability 
  Non-Infrastructure 
 
 
Brief Description: 
 
The City/State Construction (CSC) program consists of work to accommodate 
infrastructure projects by various Upstate New York municipalities, as well as the New 
York State Department of Transportation (NYSDOT).  The CSC program is directed at 
replacing gas infrastructure that will be compromised by third party construction activities. 
 
The scope of the FY2021 program includes approximately 26,523 linear feet (5.023 miles) 
of main installation to accommodate municipal capital infrastructure improvements.  The 
program will contribute approximately 5,750 linear feet (one mile) of LPP retirement to 
the Company’s Proactive Main and Service Replacement Program.  The LPP retirement 
mileage and spending estimates are based on historical information and the current 
schedule of municipal work. 
 
Program Justification: 
 
The Company’s facilities are often in direct conflict with proposed municipal infrastructure 
installations or are required to be relocated based on regulatory requirements. 
 
Niagara Mohawk’s CSC budget is divided into reimbursable and non-reimbursable 
categories.  Projects are placed into either category based on the project funding source.  
Public works projects initiated by the NYSDOT, cities, counties, and third-party private 
entities are occasionally reimbursable.  Reimbursable projects include relocation of 
existing facilities on private ROW and relocations required by private entities.  Conversely, 
non-reimbursable projects include required relocation of existing facilities that are on 
public ROW and are funded by the NYSDOT or the numerous municipalities that Niagara 
Mohawk serves.   
 
The Company’s government liaisons work closely with engineers and consultants from the 
NYSDOT and Upstate New York municipalities to minimize any direct conflicts with the 
existing gas infrastructure.  Collaborating with municipalities reduces the Company’s 
O&M costs, maximizes remuneration, and reduces risk exposure to the Company.

Program Title:  Interstate I-81 Relocation (NYSDOT) – Syracuse, NY 
 
Spending Rationale: 
 
  Mandated 
  Customer Connections 
  Reliability 
  Non-Infrastructure 
 
 
Brief Description: 
 
The purpose of the I-81 Viaduct Project is to address deterioration and outdated design 
standards of an elevated highway through the City of Syracuse. Regardless of the 
alternative selected (replacement, Community Grid, tunnel), the Company’s facilities will 
be in direct conflict with this public works project and the Company anticipates having to 
relocate significant gas infrastructure to accommodate the New York State Department of 
Transportation (NYSDOT) project.  Construction is anticipated to commence in 2021 and 
estimated to be completed in the year 2026.  
 
The NYSDOT preliminary draft design report/draft environmental impact statement for 
the I-81 Project determined that the Community Grid alternative best meets the project 
objectives.  The scope of the I-81 Community Grid alternative would involve demolishing 
the existing viaduct between the Renwick Ave railway bridge and the I-81/I-690 
interchange.   
. 
 
Program Justification: 
 
The Company’s facilities often are in direct conflict with NYSDOT projects and must be 
relocated pursuant to regulatory requirements. 
 
The NYSDOT provided preliminary engineering documents to National Grid which 
identify conflicts both on private ROW and in the municipal/NYSDOT ROW.  
Consequently, the proposed I-81 project budget has both reimbursable and non-
reimbursable work.   
 
The Company’s engineers will be working closely with the NYSDOT to minimize any 
direct conflicts with the existing gas infrastructure.  Collaborating with the NYSDOT 
reduces the Company’s O&M costs, maximizes remuneration, and reduces risk exposure 
to the Company.

Program Title:  Replace Pipe on Bridges 
 
Spending Rationale: 
 
  Mandated 
  Customer Connections 
  Reliability 
  Non-Infrastructure
 
Brief Description: 
 
This program will replace gas pipe on bridges (or any structures elevated more than four feet 
above grade) due to specific integrity concerns identified through corrosion inspections. The 
Company is proposing to replace such mains. 
Program Justification:   
 
The program is necessary because the configuration, condition, and maintenance of mains on the 
identified structures require investment in replacement that is not addressed by other programs 
due to cost and complexity. For example, the Proactive Main and Service Replacement (LPP) 
Program does not include replacement on bridges and structures and the Corrosion Control 
Program includes remediation of condition issues on structures (re-coating) but does not address 
situations where full replacements are required.

Program Title:  Proactive Main and Service Replacement (Leak Prone Pipe) 
 
Spending Rationale: 
 
  Mandated 
  Customer Connections 
  Reliability 
  Non-Infrastructure
 
Brief Description: 
 
Leak prone pipe (“LPP”) in the NMPC service territory is defined as pipe less than 16 inches in 
diameter that is (i) unprotected (i.e., non-cathodically protected) steel pipe (whether bare or 
coated), (ii) cast and wrought iron pipe, (iii) pre-1985 vintage Aldyl-A plastic pipe, and (iv) 
unprotected steel/wrought iron, copper, vintage HDPE and Aldyl-A plastic services (“associated 
services”). 
 
NMPC’s existing rate plan (Case 17-G-0239 – Proceeding on Motion of the Commission as to 
the Rates, Charges, Rules and Regulations of Niagara Mohawk Power Corporation d/b/a 
National Grid for Gas Service) requires the Company to retire a minimum of 45 miles of LPP in 
CY 2018 and CY 2019, or a cumulative three-year total of 150 miles of LPP by the end of CY 
2020.. 
 
For the reasons described below, the Company is recommending a proactive base LPP replacement 
target of 46.2 miles per year starting FY2021 and years thereafter.  Keeping the accelerated 
replacement constant at the target level will eliminate all LPP on NMPC’s system in 11 years 
starting FY2021. 
 
 
Note: *Total LPP replacement includes, miles from City State Construction and Reliability 
programs 
 
This program also includes funding to install new main and/or perform upgrades on a total of 
approximately two miles of non-leak prone distribution main per year beginning in the Rate Year 
to address low pressure issues that may result by retiring LPP in some areas. 
 
Program Justification:   
 
As of December 31, 2018, the LPP inventory includes 564 miles (205 miles of unprotected steel, 
349 miles of cast iron/wrought iron, and 10 miles of Aldyl-A), which represents approximately 6.4 
percent of the distribution system in NMPC’s territory.  The current leak repair rate for all pipe on 
the NMPC distribution system is 0.07 leaks per mile (excluding excavation leaks), which is an 
improvement from 0.29 leaks per mile in 2005.  The current LPP leak rate of 0.87 leaks per mile; 
however, is higher than the 2005 LPP leak rate of 0.48 leaks per mile but relatively stable compared 
to the 2016 and 2017 values of 0.85 and 0.72 respectively.  Since the LPP leak rate has stabilized, 
Fiscal Year 
FY2021
FY2022
FY2023
FY2024 
FY2025

--- Page 138 ---
the Company is proposing to hold the existing LPP replacement target at 50 miles per year.  This 
will reduce rate impact while still delivering the desired results.  
The proposed LPP replacement program also is supported by the Company’s Distribution Integrity 
Management Plan (DIMP) which specifies that the Company: (i) know its distribution piping 
system, (ii) understand the threats to the system, and (iii) evaluate the risks and prepare 
replacement programs for its leak prone mains and services inventory to help mitigate those risks.    
Leak predictive models show that main replacement levels below a certain threshold will cause 
leak rates to increase exponentially.  Replacement levels below this amount will cause leaks to 
increase to a point where the Company may not be able to react to the quantity of new leaks in a 
timely manner.  Furthermore, such increased leak activity will classify larger quantities of mains 
as actively corroding which will require replacement just the same.  The model shows that there is 
a practical limit to how many leaks a system can have and continue to operate safely.    
LPP is only 6.4% of the NMPC distribution main inventory yet accounts for 90% of leak repairs 
(excluding damages).  Replacement of this pipe will improve safety, reliability, and customer 
satisfaction. The key benefits of the LPP replacement program include: 
• Improved public safety by reducing the risk for gas related incidents 
• Improved system reliability and customer satisfaction 
• Compliance with federal and state code requirements, including new US Department of 
Transportation’s DIMP requirements 
• Increased efficiency resulting from reduced commodity loss 
• Reduction of methane emissions helping to reduce greenhouse gas emissions 
 
Additionally, in some areas of the Company’s service territory, retirement of LPP will result in 
low pressures on surrounding non-leak prone distribution main. The Company estimates that 
reinforcements to approximately two miles of non-leak prone main per year beginning in the Rate 
Year will be required in order to retire LPP.

costs incurred to replace LPP miles above the base target.  
 
 
 
Customer Benefit:

Customers can benefit from the program in the following ways: 
 
• Improved public safety due to reduced risk of gas incidents 
• Fewer unplanned service interruptions 
• Fewer unplanned disruptions to traffic and roadways 
• Fewer nuisance gas leaks and lowered greenhouse gas emissions 
• This program supports the State’s CLCPA goals. 
 
 
Alternatives  
 
Alternative 1: Minimal Replacement 
 
This option would replace only the quantity of main required to hold leak rates to present levels.  
This option increases safety risks and does not align with the Company’s or the Commission’s 
goals to continue to reduce leak rates.   
 
Alternative 2: Do Nothing 
 
No main replacement will result in increasing leak activity and increased risk to public safety.  
This will result in loss of credibility with regulators and put the Company in violation of its 
federally-regulated DIMP.

Program Title:  Reactive Replacement – Atmospheric Corrosion & Plastic Fusions Inspections 
 
Spending Rationale: 
 
  Mandated 
  Customer Connections 
  Reliability 
  Non-Infrastructure
 
Brief Description: 
 
This program funds the replacement of smaller segments of main and service piping to remediate 
condition issues discovered during: 1) atmospheric corrosion inspections required by state and 
federal codes, and 2) plastic fusion inspections, as well as issues discovered during the regular 
course of business. 
 
Atmospheric corrosion inspection of outside and inside services includes the visual 
inspection of the: 
 
• Service riser, soil to air interface as applicable 
• Service piping through the outlet of the meter, meter(s), regulator(s) and fittings 
• Wall penetration or point of entry as applicable 
 
This program addresses both inside and outside gas service meter locations. Inspections of 
above-grade outside piping and inside service sets are performed by Operations.  Remediation 
and repairs to substandard conditions are corrected whenever discovered.  Historically, there 
have been few issues associated with inside service inspections due to the generally protected 
environments on inside piping.  However, recent industry failures and safety concerns have 
increased awareness by gas operators of potential risks. 
 
If any plastic fusion on the gas infrastructure fails visual inspection during the normal course of 
business, its proactive replacement will remediate and enhance employee and public safety. 
 
Program Justification:   
 
Atmospheric Corrosion: Federal regulation 49 CFR 192.481 requires operators to monitor and 
inspect for evidence of atmospheric corrosion at least once every three (3) calendar years. 49 
CFR 192.479 requires operators to clean and coat each pipeline that is exposed to the atmosphere 
as/if required. 
 
Plastic Fusion: The Commission’s “Order Requiring Local Distribution Companies to Follow 
and Complete Remediation Plans as Modified by This Order and to Implement New Inspection 
Protocols” issued and effective May 15, 2015 in Case 14-G-0212, requires the Company to keep 
records of each fuse uncovered in the regular course of business and to remediate any fuse that 
fails visual inspection.

Allocating dedicated funding provides for a managed replacement program.  This program will 
improve public safety and gas system reliability.  Additional benefits highlighted include: 
• Reduction of risk associated with exposed service piping 
• Improved public and employee safety by reducing the risk for gas related incidents. 
• Enhance customer satisfaction while achieving efficiencies through integration with other 
programs (e.g. leak survey etc.) 
• Compliance with federal and state code requirements including the US Department of 
Transportation (“USDOT”) Distribution Integrity Management Program requirements 
(“DIMP”) 
• Improved public, community and government relations due to decreased odor calls 
• Improve system performance 
• Contributes positively towards the Company’s greenhouse gas reduction goals 
• This program supports the State’s CLCPA goals.

plastic fusions to be identified and repaired for consideration through the budget planning

Program Title:  Transmission Services Removal Program 
 
Spending Rationale: 
 
  Mandated 
  Customer Connections 
  Reliability 
  Non-Infrastructure
 
Brief Description: 
 
Niagara Mohawk had 260 services connected to Transmission Pipeline E-31 in Saratoga County.  
Pipeline E-31 has a maximum allowable operating pressure of 200 psig.  Recently, the Company 
adopted new process safety standards for transmission assets in accordance with API 1173.  The 
current design and construction of these transmission services no longer meet the Company’s 
internal process safety standards.  The Company began a five-year program in FY2019 that will 
permanently remove these high-risk services from transmission mains and transfer these services 
to existing or new distribution main.  In the last two years, the Company retired 48 transmission 
services along E-31.  In FY2021, the Company is on schedule to abandon an additional 90 
transmission services.  At the beginning of the Rate Year, the Company will have approximately 
122 services connected to Transmission Pipeline.  Once this work is completed, the Company 
will not have any services connected to transmission mains. 
 
 
Program Justification:   
 
Risk Analysis 
 
As part of its Process Safety Management Program, the Company performed a Process Hazards 
Analysis (“PHA”) to determine the overall process safety risk associated with 
the E-31 services and concluded that the cumulative safety risk exceeded the Company’s internal 
process safety risk threshold.  The PHA utilized a Layers of Protection Analysis (“LOPA”) 
technique developed by the Center for Chemical Process Safety (“CCPS”).  The study included 
scenarios such as third-party damage, pressure regulator failure, vehicular damage to above-
ground equipment, debris in the gas, and weather-related failures.  The analysis also included 
common cause failures that simultaneously would damage the regulator and its internal relief 
valve.  The study concluded that the cumulative process safety risk exceeded the Company’s 
“Broadly Acceptable” risk profile due to insufficient layers of protection to manage the risk of 
transmission pressure reaching the customer premises.  The customer service regulators are not 
rated for full transmission pressure and do not satisfy the CCPS criteria for an independent layer 
of protection, leaving the design in the “Broadly Unacceptable” risk region of National Grid’s 
risk matrix. 
 
Proposed Course of Action 
 
While the Company has previously implemented process safety upgrades to these 
services, the Company is increasingly concerned with regard to their overall risk profile and has 
concluded it is necessary to significantly reduce the overall operating risks by 
transferring these services to distribution main. 
Exhibit__(GIOP-4) 
Page 25 of 136


--- Page 143 ---
 
Of the current 122 transmission services, 7 are within 200 feet of an existing distribution 
pipeline.  Approximately 3.6 miles of new distribution pipeline will be required to provide a 
source of gas supply to the remaining transmission service customers.  The 
program involves installing new distribution pipelines, new distribution rated service 
lines, and the decommissioning, abandoning and securing of each transmission service 
tee, including the use of custom mechanical coupling to secure abandon transmission 
service tees thereby avoiding costly welding procedures, non-destructive examination, 
hydrostatic testing, and multiple excavation openings.

Customer Benefit:   
 
This program will ensure that residential customers currently served from Pipeline E-31 
continue to receive reliable gas service while improving safety through lowering of risks 
associated with the operation of the Company’s equipment.  This program will thus improve 
public safety and gas system reliability. 
 
Alternatives  
 
Alternative 1: Downgrade Pipeline E-31 to 124psi 
 
Downgrading Pipeline E-31 to 124psi is not feasible because, at 124psi, the pipeline 
cannot meet the customer gas load demand. 
 
Alternative 2: Construct new transmission line and downgrade Pipeline E-31 to 
distribution pressure 
 
This option would be very expensive.  Furthermore, a new transmission main for that line 
is not in the scheduled projects and would take several years to design and complete. 
 
Alternative 3: Upgrade transmission services to current Company process safety 
Standards 
 
This option will avoid the necessity of transferring service connections to nearby 
distribution pipelines and the need to construct new distribution pipelines.  Above ground 
pressure regulating equipment would need to be replaced with more robust equipment, 
and new steel underground service lines would need to be installed to the customer 
services.  Additionally, the annual inspection program would need to be continued for the 
life of the services.

Alternative 4: Do Nothing 
 
This alternative would leave all the transmission services attached to Pipeline E-31 in 
their current state.  This alternative fails to mitigate the operational risks identified 
through the PHA and LOPA analyses.

Program Title: Access Restrictions on Elevated Gas Infrastructure 
 
Spending Rationale: 
 
 Mandated 
  Customer Connections 
 Reliability 
 Non-Infrastructure
 
Brief Description: 
 
The Company is proposing a program to reduce the risk of public injury by restricting and/or 
deterring public access to the Company’s elevated gas facilities.  
 
Where required, the Company is proposing to install barriers on elevated infrastructure to restrict 
the public from easy access. Further, the Company will install signage that will be easily noticeable 
and will most likely deter public from accessing our elevated infrastructures. 
 
Program Justification:    
 
The purpose of this program is to reduce the risk of climb and fall injuries or fatalities.  In 2014 
in the United Kingdom, a fatality occurred resulting from a climb and fall accident on an 
elevated gas pipeline at a bridge crossing operated by a National Grid affiliate company.   
 
Currently, the Company has approximately 374 locations where exposed gas pipelines are four 
feet or higher above the ground or across a body of water.  It has been determined that 158 of 
these locations require barriers or deterrents to discourage the public from climbing or accessing 
the facilities.  The program installed fencing or other physical deterrents at 42 of these locations 
previously.  The remaining 116 locations will be remediated by the end of the rate case.

Program Title:  Service Replacement (Reactive Non-Leaks)  
 
Spending Rationale: 
 
  Mandated 
  Customer Connections 
  Reliability 
Non-Infrastructure
 
Brief Description: 
 
The reactive non-leak service replacement program consists of non-discretionary work 
that is randomly generated through compliance needs and mandated activities.     
 
Program Justification:   
 
The goal of this program is to enhance safety and reliability of the Company’s system by 
reducing the risks associated with damages, service abandonments due to inactivity or 
demolition requests, customer driven relocations of existing services, and other 
substandard conditions.  The program provides approved funding for the reactive 
replacement of gas services to address non-leak work activities that fall outside the 
normal scope of the integrity, reliability, public works and growth programs.   
 
The proactive main and service replacement programs upgrade existing customer services 
prioritized in the main replacement program by risk based on pressure, material, vintage, 
location, and select other variables.  The reactive service replacement program addresses 
the responses to correct deficiencies on remaining services. 
 
The program budget consists of costs to replace as a result of damages, service 
abandonments due to inactivity or demolition requests, customer driven relocations of 
existing services, and other substandard conditions.

Program Title:  Main Replacement (Reactive) 
 
Spending Rationale: 
 
  Mandated 
  Customer Connections 
  Reliability 
  Non-Infrastructure
 
Brief Description: 
 
This program will fund the replacement of smaller sections of main segments and associated 
services that are identified during leak repairs that cannot be repaired by leak clamps. This 
program allows Field Operations the ability to make decisions quickly on site to replace actively 
deteriorating segments of pipe. The program covers the Company’s inventory of Leak Prone 
Pipe (“LPP”) as defined elsewhere. 
 
Program Justification:   
 
The goal of this program is to replace small sections of actively corroded mains quickly and 
reduce the risk associated with LPP in the Company’s distribution system. The replacement of 
LPP and associated services also is supported by the Company’s Distribution Integrity 
Management Plan (“DIMP”), which specifies that the Company implement measures to know its 
system, understand the threats to its distribution piping system, and evaluate risks and prepare 
replacement programs to help mitigate the risks associated with its leak prone mains and services 
inventory.

Program Title:  Low Pressure System Elimination Program 
 
Spending Rationale: 
 
  Mandated 
  Customer Connections 
  Reliability 
  Non-Infrastructure
 
Brief Description: 
 
The Company is proposing to systematically retire two low pressure systems per year, one in each 
of its East and Central divisions, by transferring customers currently on those systems to medium 
pressure (MP) beginning in the Rate Year. 
 
Program Justification:   
 
The purpose of this program is to increase the safety of Customers on the Company’s 
distribution system.  Customer premises within a traditional LP system are protected from over 
pressurization by district regulator stations.  There typically are not regulators or excess flow 
valves at each Customer premise like there are in a MP system. 
 
The Merrimac Valley Incident in Massachusetts that occurred in September 2018 provided an 
example of what can happen when problems at a district regulator station lead to 
overpressurization of an entire LP system. 
 
This program will transfer all Customers on the selected LP systems to a nearby MP system by 
installing new distribution main and services.  The new MP services will be installed to current 
standards with excess flow valves and regulators at each Customer premise. 
 
The Low Pressure System Elimination program will initially target systems that meet the 
following criteria: 
 
• At least one Regulator Station within the LP system has been identified for replacement 
or refurbishment 
o Replacement of these systems under this program will allow for funds to be spent 
improving Regulator Station safety elsewhere within the System 
• Main inventory contains less than 10% of leak prone pipe (LPP) 
o Systems containing small amounts of LPP will have the lowest likelihood of main 
replacement under current Main Replacement and Low Pressure Zones programs 
 
The list of systems that have been identified are tabulate below.

This option does not allow the company to begin to systematically eliminate the safety concerns 
associated with LP systems that have been brought to light by the September 2018 Merrimac 
Valley incident.

Program Title:   Contractor Safety Inspections 
 
Spending Rationale: 
 
  Mandated 
  Customer Connections 
  Reliability 
  Non-Infrastructure 
 
 
Brief Description: 
 
This program will expand the current Contractor Safety/Compliance Inspections Program 
so that there is an inspector assigned to every contractor main and service project on the 
Company’s system.  A three-year plan will be needed to ensure successful implementation.       
 
 
Program Justification 
 
The current Contractor Safety/Compliance Inspector Program is staffed to provide one 
inspector to cover approximately 3.5 contractor crews.  The contractor safety/compliance 
inspectors perform site inspection functions including but not limited to the following:  
Operator Qualification verification, jobsite and safe work practices, work methods and 
construction standards/procedure compliance, work scope documentation, and customer 
interaction if necessary. 
 
To implement safety process improvements, and in line with best practices deployed by 
other utilities operating in similar regions, the Company proposes adding resources to 
ensure that there is one inspector to cover every contractor project.  In the service territory, 
21 additional contractor inspectors and 5 supervisors are required.  The 
hiring/implementation plan spans 3 years.

Program Title: Integrity Verification Process Program 
 
Spending Rationale: 
 
  Mandated 
  Customer 
Connections 
  Reliability 
 Non-Infrastructure
 
Brief Description: 
 
This program covers projects related to the US Department of Transportation’s rules on 
Integrity Verification Process (IVP) programs.  The renewed Pipeline Safety Act of 2011 
mandates that the Pipeline and Hazardous Materials Safety Administration (PHMSA) 
establish rules requiring operators to demonstrate their pipelines are “Fit For Service.”  
This includes reviewing existing records to determine if prior strength tests (hydro static 
pressure tests) were completed at the time of construction, as well as other records that 
prove the pipeline is operating within design parameters.  On January 10, 2011, PHMSA 
issued advisory bulletin ADB-11-01 directing operators to conduct a comprehensive 
records review and verification prior to issue of the final rule making. The first part of the 
rule was adopted in a final rulemaking on October 1, 2019 (“Final Rulemaking”), with an 
effective date of July 1, 2020 
 
The Company  proposes an IVP Program that incorporates the elements of the rulemaking 
and PHMSA guidance document ADB-11-01 along with records review, pipeline 
replacement, the retirement of non-essential pipeline segments, and Critical Engineering 
Assesments (ECA).  This program will better enable the Company to comply with 
regulatory requirements of the first and second parts of the current rulemaking. 
 
Program Justification: 
   
In 2016, PHMSA issued a Notice of Proposed Rulemaking (NOPR) that will address the 
2011 Pipeline Safety Act mandates and implement a number of additional changes to the 
regulations for gas pipelines. Among the proposals are the establishment of maximum 
allowable operating pressure (MAOP) and testing mandates for existing pipelines.  
PHMSA has proposed eliminating the exemption clause for establishing the MAOP of pre-
1970 “grandfathered” pipe which allows certain pipelines to operate at the highest actual 
operating pressure to which they were subjected during the five years prior to July 1, 1970 
without having to perform a pressure test.  PHMSA has also proposed that all pipelines not 
previously pressure tested at or above 1.1 times MAOP should be required to be pressure 
tested in accordance with current regulations.  Another initiative proposed is PHMSA’s 
IVP, which will require operators lacking certain records to conduct pressure tests to 
confirm MAOP, and require operators with missing records, inadequately validated or 
traceable material documentation (TVC) to design and implement a program to establish 
material properties by one or more of the following methods: (1) cutting out and testing 
pipe samples; (2) institute non-destructive testing; (3) field verification of code stamp for 
components such as valves, flanges, and fabrications; or (4) other verifications. 
 
Exhibit__(GIOP-4) 
Page 40 of 136


--- Page 158 ---
 
 
Some pipelines without adequate material and pressure test documentation will be required 
to be retired or replaced.  The IVP will also require an operator to develop a “Fit for Service 
Program” to establish that all pipelines are operating within their design parameters.  On 
January 10, 2011 PHMSA issued advisory bulletin ADB-11-01 directing operators to 
conduct a comprehensive records review and verification prior to issue of the final rule 
making.  
 
The NRPM was adopted in a final rulemaking on October 1, 2019 (“Final Rulemaking”), 
with an effective date of July 1, 2020.  Because the Company’s IMP/IVP workplan is risk 
based, and in light of PHMSA’s new regulations expanding IMP & IVP, the Company 
believes that its proposed IVP program is a reasonable and conservative approach to 
managing pipeline integrity during the Rate Year and Data Years. 
 
 
 
The rule requires  Operators to: 
 
• To provide pressure test records for pipelines  that were previously   Such pipelines 
will now be subject to hydrostatic testing requirements. 
• Require operators to confirm the records they use to justify MAOP (TVC)  
• Re-Hydro test pipe segments  
• Run in-line inspection tools (ILI)  
• Abandon/retire pipelines  
• Replace pipelines  
• Material sampling to establish properties oppurtuunistaically  
• Advance fit for service analysis  (ECA)  
• Enhanced internal corrosion monitoring

reduction or public safety.  This approach also fails to account for the  impact of non 
compliance the new rule making.  The established regulation time frame will require  
accelerated project and assessment schedules.  Accordingly, there is a risk of not meeting

not meeting deadline requirements.

Program Title:  Pipeline 16 Pipe Segment Replacement 
 
Spending Rationale: 
 
  Mandated 
  Customer Connections 
  Reliability 
  Non-Infrastructure
 
Brief Description: 
 
This project proposes the replacement of approximately seven miles (37,000 feet) of DOT 
classified transmission pipeline that has an original pressure test no longer appropriate for the 
current class location.  This pipe will be replaced with new pressure tested 24” pipe that meets the 
current class location in accordance with 16NYCRR Part 255.611.  Pipeline 16 has an MAOP of 
473 psig.  The segments to be replaced are located in three areas, Chittenango, Cicero, and Clay, 
NY.  The project is planned over eight years beginning in 2020.   
 
Year One (FY 2020-21): 
• Complex Capital Delivery process 
• Detailed Project scope, test holes as necessary 
• Preliminary design 
• Begin permitting 
 
Year Two (FY 2021-22): 
• Continue design for piping replacement. 
• Continue permitting, land acquisition and material procurement. 
• Order long lead materials 
 
Year Three (FY 2022-23): 
• Continue design for piping replacement. 
• Continue permitting, land acquisition and material procurement.   
 
Year Four (FY 2023-24): 
• Begin installation of approximately 35,000 feet of new 24-inch pipe in the same 
right-of-way (ROW) parallel to the existing 24-inch piping (start first installation 
project near Orange Commons in the Town of Clay). 
• Continue design for piping replacement.

Project Justification 
 
In response to a class location study required by16 NYCRR Part 255.609 and 255.611, the 
Company identified 35,000 feet of pipe where the original pressure test is insufficient for the 
current class location.  The original design class location of “2” was based on population present 
during design and construction in 1958.  Recent population increase and construction of new 
structures close to the pipeline has resulted in a change to the class location.  In accordance with 
the requirements of the referenced code sections, this pipeline must either have its MAOP reduced 
or be replaced with new pipe and tested to meet the new class location design requirements.

Program Title:  Pipeline E18 Replacement of Pipeline 
 
Spending Rationale: 
 
  Mandated 
  Customer Connections 
  Reliability 
  Non-Infrastructure
 
Brief Description: 
 
This project proposes the replacement of approximately 27 miles of DOT classified transmission 
pipeline that has been maintaining its maximum allowable operating pressure (MAOP) based on 
the “Grandfather Clause” in 49 CFR 192.619(a)(3), i.e., the highest historical actual operating 
pressure in the five years preceding 1970.  The project will replace existing 16”, 20” and 24” pipe 
with new pressure tested 24” pipe.  Pipeline E18 has an MAOP of 490 psig.  The piping to be 
replaced begins near the Putnam Road Gate Station and proceeds northwest until it reaches the 
Loughberry Lake Gas Regulating Station near the City of Saratoga Springs, NY.  The project is 
planned over nine years beginning in 2021.

 Program Title:  Integrity Management Program 
 
Spending Rationale: 
 
  Mandated 
  Customer Connections 
  Reliability 
 Non-Infrastructure
 
Brief Description: 
 
This program covers projects related to the management of the Company’s gas 
transmission system, specifically the O&M and capital projects that are components of the 
US Department of Transportation’s (DOT) mandated Integrity Management Program 
(IMP).   
 
The Pipeline Safety Improvement Act of 2002 (“2002 Act”) requires operators of DOT-
reportable gas transmission systems to develop and implement an IMP for all pipelines 
operating above 20 percent specified minimum yield strength (SMYS) in a high 
consequence area (HCA).  The Pipeline Safety, Regulatory Certainty, and Job Creation Act 
of 2011 (“2011 Act”) mandates that Pipeline and Hazardous Material Safety 
Administration (PHMSA) consider whether the existing transmission IMP should be 
expanded beyond the current requirements, including increased inspections of IMP-
covered pipelines using in-line inspection (ILI) technology. 
 
In May 2016, PHMSA issued a Notice of Proposed Rulemaking (“NPRM”) that proposes 
new pipeline safety regulations that include a requirement for increased inspection of IMP-
covered pipelines utilizing ILI technology and an expanded definition of Medium 
Consequence Areas (MCA), as well as a host of other requirements.  To meet these 
requirements, transmission pipelines must be ILI enabled where possible. 
 
The NRPM was adopted in a final rulemaking on October 1, 2019 (“Final Rulemaking”), 
with an effective date of July 1, 2020.  Because the Company’s IMP workplan is risk based, 
and in light of PHMSA’s new regulations expanding IMP, the Company believes that its 
proposed IMP program is a reasonable and conservative approach to managing pipeline 
integrity during the Rate Year and Data Years. 
 
 
The Company proposes an updated IMP that incorporates the elements of the current IMP 
along with proactive programs such as retrofitting pipelines for ILI including free 
swimming, robotic and tethered tools.  The proposed IMP enhancements provide the 
greatest amount of risk reduction, thereby improving system safety and reliability.   
 
Program Justification:  
  
Pursuant to the 2002 Act, the DOT promulgated rules on managing the integrity of 
transmission pipelines used by the gas and hazardous liquids industries under 49 CFR Part 
192.901 – 192.951, which became effective on January 14, 2004.  These regulations require 
pipeline operators to develop and implement an IMP for “covered” transmission pipelines, 
Exhibit__(GIOP-4) 
Page 54 of 136


--- Page 172 ---
which are defined as certain pipelines in high consequence areas (HCA).  The program 
required that the first cycle of pipeline assessments be completed no later than 2012.  
Reassessments are required to be completed at intervals not exceeding seven years 
thereafter from the last assessment.  The assessments are comprised of external corrosion 
direct assessment (ECDA) and ILI.  The results of each operator’s program are summarized 
and reported to the DOT on an annual basis. 
 
Pipeline safety laws and regulations constantly evolve driving progressive changes in 
utility operations and asset management.  San Bruno and several other high-profile pipeline 
incidents have set in motion recommendations, proposed rulemaking, and the 2011 Act 
signed into law on January 3, 2012.  The final published rule (first part & pending second 
part) has the following significant items that will affect the IMP: 
 
• ILI enable all pipeline segments operating at or over 20 percent SMYS  
• Develop requirements for medium consequence areas (MCA)  
• Reduce or eliminate the use of ECDA  
• Require advanced risk modeling, including quantitative assessments 
• Enhanced anomaly repair criteria  
• Revise definition of “Transmission Pipeline” 
• Develop a “Crack Like Flaw” evaluation program  
• Enhanced gas quality monitoring program  
 
Proposed rulemaking by PHMSA in response to the 2011 Act became final with the first 
part of the rule published on October 1st 2019, with an effective date of July 1st ,2020.  It 
is anticipated that the second part of the rule will be published the 4th quarter of 2020.

Program Title:  Transmission Station Integrity Program 
 
Spending Rationale: 
 
  Mandated 
  Customer Connections 
  Reliability 
 Non-Infrastructure
 
 
Brief Description: 
 
This program covers capital projects involving the pressure regulating facilities and heaters 
utilized on the Company’s gas system and related to the US Department of Transportation’s 
rulemaking on Pipeline Safety: Safety of Gas Transmission and Gathering Pipelines.  The 
renewed Pipeline Safety Acts of 2011 & 2016 mandate that Pipeline and Hazardous Materials 
Safety Administration (PHMSA) establish rules requiring operators to validate and reconfirm the 
Maximum Allowable Operating Pressure (MAOP) of their pipelines.  This includes reviewing 
existing records to determine if prior strength tests (hydro static pressure tests) were completed at 
the time of construction, as well as other records that prove the pipeline is operating within 
design parameters.  On January 10, 2011, PHMSA issued advisory bulletin ADB-11-01 directing 
operators to conduct a comprehensive records review and verification prior to issue of the final 
rule making.  On August 25, 2011, PHMSA issued an Advance Notice of Proposed Rulemaking 
regarding regulations governing the safety of gas transmission pipelines, and on April 8, 2016, 
PHMSA issued a corresponding Notice of Proposed Rulemaking to revise the Pipeline Safety 
Regulations applicable to the safety of onshore gas transmission and gathering pipelines.  
Finally, on October 1, 2019, USDOT issued the final rules relative to the proposed rulemakings, 
with an effective date of July 1, 2020.  The mandates, advisory bulletin, notices, and final rules 
also apply to transmission-connected pressure regulating stations and take stations (hereafter, 
‘stations’).  The Company proposes this Transmission Station Integrity Program (TSIP), 
consisting of projects which focus on the rehabilitation, partial replacement, or full replacement 
of stations that do not meet the requirements of MAOP reconfirmation as set forth in the final 
rule. 
 
 
Program Justification:   
 
The October 1, 2019 issuance of the final rules by PHMSA addresses the 2011 Pipeline Safety 
Act mandates and implements a number of additional changes to the regulations for stations.  
Among the changes, are requirements for the validation and reconfirmation of maximum 
allowable operating pressure (MAOP) and testing mandates for existing facilities.  Additionally, 
in certain conditions, PHMSA has eliminated the exemption clause for establishing the MAOP of 
pre-1970 “grandfathered” pipe, which allows certain stations to operate at the highest actual 
operating pressure to which they were subjected during the five years prior to July 1, 1970, 
without having to perform a pressure test.  In order to validate and reconfirm MAOP, the 
Company has instituted an Integrity Verification Program (IVP) for stations, which aims to 
review the station-specific records and identify gaps that prohibit verification of MAOP.  The 
final rules require operators with missing records, inadequately validated or traceable material 
documentation (TVC) to design and implement a program to establish material properties by one 
Exhibit__(GIOP-4) 
Page 61 of 136


--- Page 179 ---
or more of the following methods: (1) cutting out and testing pipe samples; (2) In situ non-
destructive testing; (3) field verification of code stamp for components such as valves, flanges, 
and fabrications; or (4) other verifications. 
 
The rulemaking establishes three deadlines for compliance.  By July 1, 2021, the Company must 
develop and document the procedures and plans needed for compliance.  By July 3, 2028, the 
Company must attain 50 percent compliance among its inventory of transmission assets.  By July 
2, 2035, the Company must attain 100 percent compliance for all transmission assets. 
 
A portion of stations will not meet the requirements set forth for MAOP reconfirmation and will 
be required to be retired or replaced.  The proposed Transmission Station Integrity Program 
(TSIP) will address these stations through a variety of projects including the rehabilitation of 
existing stations, the partial replacement of station components, or the complete replacement of 
entire stations, as determined on a case-by-case basis.  As system performance and reliability 
allow, some stations may be retired rather than replaced.  The Company has an inventory of 165 
transmission-connected stations, and the Transmission Station Integrity Program will ensure that 
they all meet the compliance deadlines laid out above. 
 
 
Total Project Cost Breakdown:

Program Title: Valve Installation and Replacement Program 
 
Spending Rationale: 
 
  Mandated 
  Customer Connections 
  Reliability 
Non-Infrastructure
 
 
Brief Description: 
 
The Valve Installation and Replacement Program addresses valve replacements 
necessitated by ongoing annual inspections in addition to new valve installations that 
enhance system resiliency.  This program will strengthen the emergency response 
capabilities of the gas organization by improving the level at which Field Operations can 
safely and efficiently isolate sections of the distribution system while ensuring minimum 
customer impact and reducing the duration of future outages.   
 
Program Justification:   
 
Federal (49 CFR 192.181) and state (16 NYCRR 255.181) regulations require the 
Company to install, inspect, maintain and operate critical pipeline valves on all gas 
distribution systems.  These valves facilitate the rapid shutdown of distribution piping or 
regulator stations during gas emergencies such as third-party damage, water intrusion, or 
other operational reasons.  The valves also facilitate maintenance and pipe replacement 
activities on associated distribution piping.  Ensuring all critical valves are properly 
maintained and operable is a key public safety function and is critical to the effective 
operation of the Company’s gas distribution system. 
 
The Company’s Instrumentation and Regulation group is responsible for performing 
annual valve inspections and any resulting repair and/or replacement work identified 
through the inspections.  Program status and compliance is reported monthly.  Gas Asset 
Management has enterprise-wide responsibility for the Valve Installation and 
Replacement Program.  This includes valve selection criteria and determination, as well 
as development of system isolation districts.  The Strategic Asset and System Planning 
and Project Engineering & Design teams also provide ongoing support to Field 
Operations through diagnosis of inoperable valves, identification of alternate valves and 
selection of new valves.

Successful execution of the program will ensure the safety and reliability of the gas assets 
while focusing on improvements in customer satisfaction.  The primary driver for this 
program is to improve distribution system and customer reliability while maintaining the 
highest standards for safety of the gas distribution assets.  The program will minimize the 
unplanned release of gas during restoration of damage to Company facilities. 
 
This program supports the State’s CLCPA goals. 
 
Alternatives: 
 
 
Alternative 1:  Do Nothing 
 
The valves found to be deficient will need to be managed on a case by case basis, 
creating process and investment inefficiencies.  Failure to properly plan and employ 
uniform criteria to these issues increases risk to the Company and can portray a negative 
image of the organization to customers, investors and regulators. 
 
 
Studies/References That Support the Program: 
 
1. Outage Restoration Costs Study 
 
Estimates for relighting customers and recovering from a system outage have been 
prepared to quantify the impact of outages related to insufficient system capacity 
during periods of peak demand and severe winter cold. 
 
Actual relight costs have been captured from recent incidents to quantify company 
expenses related to restoring service.  These were all related to outages that occurred 
for reasons other than insufficient system capacity and operations were conducted 
under benign weather conditions.  It is likely that during severe winter weather, costs 
would increase. 
 
The claims data related to burst pipes and equipment damage due to a lack of heat 
during severe cold weather was captured from National Grid incidents in other 
jurisdictions.  The combined cost of relighting customers and resolving claims in 
those incidents averaged $1,764 per customer.   Recognizing the amount of variability 
in different incidents such as weather conditions, different types of neighborhoods, 
variable labor costs, economies of scale, etc., for purposes of evaluating the benefits 
of reinforcement projects, an average value of service restoration costs and claims of 
$1,000 per customer is used.   
 
2.  Critical valve anomalies found during inspections: 
 
Exhibit__(GIOP-4) 
Page 65 of 136


--- Page 183 ---
 
 
The attached photo provides a recent example demonstrating the necessity of this 
program.  The underground pipeline valve shown in the photo was inspected and 
determined to be leaking natural gas to atmosphere.  Further investigation 
determined that the upper body casting had cracked, allowing gas to leak through.  
The valve had been in service for approximately 50 years.  The photo shows the 
top of the valve coated with a temporary sealant to stop the leak until preparations 
could be completed to replace the valve.  No acceptable permanent repair was 
possible.   
 
Defective valves pose several risks including potential uncontrolled leakage of 
gas to atmosphere, malfunctions during operation that prevent technicians from 
being able to control gas system emergencies, and potential injury to technicians 
when valves do not operate as designed.  
 
The valve program provides the necessary funding to ensure valves can be 
replaced as necessary when routine inspection results determine the valve is not 
operating as designed.

Program Title: Sectionalizing District Improvement Program 
 
Spending Rationale: 
 
  Mandated 
  Customer Connections 
  Reliability 
Non-Infrastructure
 
 
Brief Description: 
 
The Sectionalizing District Improvement Program addresses installation of Critical Valves 
which can be used in combination to isolate a pre-determined area of the distribution 
system.  The program will strengthen the emergency response capabilities of the gas 
organization by improving the efficiency by which the distribution system can be isolated.  
It will ensure minimum customer impact and will benefit customers by reducing the 
duration and controlling the size of future outages. 
 
Program Justification: 
 
Federal (49 CFR 192.181) and state (16 NYCRR 255.181) regulations require the 
Company to install valves on the distribution system spaced so as to reduce the time to 
shut down a section of main in an emergency.  The valve spacing is determined by the 
operating pressure, the size of the mains, and the local physical conditions as well as the 
number and type of consumers that might be affected by a shutdown. 
 
These valves facilitate the rapid shutdown of distribution piping or regulator stations 
during gas emergencies such as third-party damage, water intrusion, insufficient supply 
or other operational reasons.  Ensuring all critical valves are properly located is a key 
public safety function and is critical to the effective operation of the Company’s gas 
distribution system. 
 
The Company has revised its approach to defining Sectionalizing Districts considering 
recent large-scale outages in other jurisdictions and available resources that can be utilized 
during an incident.  This re-baselining of the Sectionalizing Districts will improve customer 
reliability because it will reduce the number of impacted customer accounts affected by a 
shutdown and/or shorten the anticipated duration of a shutdown should it occur.
